Title: Root Systems, Tits Cones and Imaginary Cones of Brink-Howlett Groupoids

URL Source: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2606.12937v1

Published Time: Fri, 12 Jun 2026 00:32:26 GMT

Number of Pages: 77

Markdown Content:
ROOT SYSTEMS, TITS CONES AND IMAGINARY CONES OF BRINK-HOWLETT GROUPOIDS 

MATTHEW DYER AND HARRISON GIMENEZ 

Abstract. We extend the basic theory of the groupoids introduced by Brink and Howlett in their study of normalizers of parabolic subgroups of Coxeter groups, by studying both their abstract root systems and root systems real-ized in real vector spaces. Such root systems have some properties formally analogous to those of root systems of Borcherds-Kac-Moody Lie algebras; in particular, some contain imaginary simple roots. Further, positive roots corre-spond to certain reflection subgroups. We also extend the most basic properties of the Tits cone and imaginary cone of Coxeter groups to corresponding cones defined for Brink-Howlett groupoids. The results linearize the study of cer-tain classes of reflection subgroups of Coxeter groups in a similar way as root systems of Coxeter groups linearize the study of reflections.

Introduction 

Let ( W, S ) be a Coxeter system with standard root system Φ. The subgroups generated by subsets of the set S of simple reflections are called standard parabolic subgroups, and their conjugates are called parabolic subgroups. Both types of subgroups are important in the study of Coxeter groups and in areas in which Coxeter groups find application, such as in the structure and representation theory of semisimple algebraic groups and Kac-Moody groups and Lie algebras. In their study [9] of normalizers of standard parabolic subgroups, Brink and Howlett studied the components of a groupoid in which the objects are the subsets 

Date : June 12, 2026. 2020 Mathematics Subject Classification. 20F55; 17B22, 20L05 . 

Key words and phrases. Coxeter groups, root systems, Brink-Howlett groupoids.  

> 1
> arXiv:2606.12937v1 [math.GR] 11 Jun 2026 2M. DYER AND H. GIMENEZ

of the simple roots and a morphism J → K is given by an element w ∈ W satisfying 

w(J) = K. We will call a component (or union of components) of this groupoid a Brink Howlett groupoid. This paper considers various notions of root systems of Brink-Howlett groupoids. We describe both abstract root systems and root systems realized as subsets of real vector spaces. The most interesting and useful of these root systems have a set of roots based at each object J of the groupoid, with the positive roots based at J

corresponding to a set of corank one reflection overgroups (in W ) of the standard parabolic subgroup WJ with J as its set of simple roots. The study of these root systems leads to study of versions “relative to J” of various basic facts about Coxeter systems which were previously known in the case J = ∅. For example, Tits theorem that a finite subgroup of a Coxeter group is contained in a finite parabolic subgroup is generalized to the statement that a finite index overgroup of WJ in W is contained in a parabolic subgroup of W which is also a finite index overgroup of WJ .Such root systems in real vector spaces linearize the study of certain classes of re-flection subgroups in a similar way as root systems linearize the study of reflections. We give a direct elementary proof that a Brink-Howlett groupoid has certain favor-able lattice theoretic properties (in regard to its weak orders, defined by inclusion of inversion sets in its root system) which may be summarized by the statement that it underlies a principal rootoid (see e.g. [19]) and to prove that the Brink-Howlett generators of Brink-Howlett groupoids are the specialization to those groupoids of the standard (simple) generators of principal rootoids. This makes the extensive (but largely unpublished) general theory of principal rootoids directly applicable to the study of Brink-Howlett groupoids, and provides interesting and important examples for that general theory. Some of the root systems we consider have certain features formally analogous to those of root systems of Borcherds-Kac-Moody Lie algebras (see [32]). In particular, they may contain imaginary simple roots, in a natural sense. In the case of root systems realized in real vector spaces, inclusion of such imaginary simple roots in the root system is necessary in order to ensure that each positive real root is expressible as a non-negative linear combination of simple roots and thereby to generalize the basic notion of root coefficient. We also define, for suitable root systems of Brink-Howlett groupoids in real vector spaces, natural analogues of the Tits cone and imaginary cone of a Coxeter group. The definitions involve both real and imaginary roots. The basic fact that the stabilizer of a subset of the fundamental chamber of the Tits cone of a Coxeter group is a standard parabolic subgroup is generalized to the fact that the “pointwise stabilizer groupoid” of a subset of a fundamental chamber of a Brink-Howlett groupoid’s Tits cone is another Brink-Howlett groupoid, which one may view as an analogue for Brink-Howlett groupoids of a standard parabolic subgroup. This paper emphasizes basic results and phenomena for Brink-Howlett groupoids for which there are no extensions known (or in some cases, possible) to principal rootoids in general. The focus is mainly on imaginary roots and realizations of the root systems in real vector spaces. The results suggest many natural directions for further study of Brink-Howlett (and other) groupoids with root systems, and applications to the study of Coxeter groups. ROOT SYSTEMS OF BRINK-HOWLETT GROUPOIDS 3

As references for elementary background we use from convex geometry, see for instance [39], [6, Ch II, §2] and [2]. 1.2. By a real quadratic space , we shall mean in this paper a pair ( V, B ) consisting of a real vector space V and a symmetric bilinear form B : V × V → R.Fix a real quadratic space ( V, B ). Let O = O( V, B ) denote the orthogonal group 

of ( V, B ), which is the group of automorphisms of ( V, B ). The elements of O are the invertible linear maps g : V → V which preserve the form B, in the sense that 

B(gx, gy ) = B(x, y ) for all x, y ∈ V . An element α of V is said to be non-isotropic 

if B(α, α )̸ = 0. For non-isotropic α ∈ V , write α∨ := 2 

> B(α,α )

α and define the 

reflection sα ∈ O by sα(v) = v − B(v, α ∨)α for v ∈ V .1.3. Let (Φ , Π) be a based root system in ( V, B ) in the sense of [24, 2.3], with positive roots Φ + and associated Coxeter system ( W, S ). This means that: (i) ( V, B ) is a real quadratic space (ii) Π ⊆ V satisfies cone(Π) ∩ cone( −Π) = {0}.(iii) for all α ∈ Π, one has α̸ ∈ cone(Π \ { α}) and B(α, α ) = 1. (iv) S := {sα | α ∈ Π} and W := ⟨S⟩ is the subgroup of O generated by S.(v) Φ := {wα | w ∈ W, α ∈ Π} satisfies Φ = Φ + ∪ Φ− where Φ + := Φ ∩ cone(Π) and Φ − := Φ ∩ cone( −Π). These conditions imply that Π is positively independent . That is, if P 

> α∈Π

cαα =0 with cα ≥ 0 for all α ∈ Π, then cα = 0 for all α.See [24], [4] and [22] for more details. For example, Φ could be the standard root system of ( W, S ), with simple roots Π forming a basis of V and B the standard symmetric bilinear form on V , as described in [5], [31], [3] and [1]. We assume the reader is familiar with basic facts concerning Coxeter systems and their root systems, as may be found in those references. 1.4. Let T := {wsw −1 | w ∈ W, s ∈ S} = { sα | α ∈ Φ }. Elements of Π, Φ, Φ +,Φ−, T and S are called simple roots , roots , positive roots , negative roots , reflections 

and simple reflections , respectively. 4 M. DYER AND H. GIMENEZ 

By definition, the rank of the Coxeter system ( W, S ) is |S|. From [20], for example, the rank is the minimum of the cardinalities of sets of reflections which generate W . Unless otherwise stated, Coxeter systems are not assumed in this paper to be of finite rank. The standard length function of ( W, S ) is denoted ℓ or ℓ(W,S ). The map α 7 →

sα : Φ + → T is a bijection. For w ∈ W , the left inversion set Φw of w is (1.4.1) Φw := Φ + ∩ w(−Φ+).

Define (1.4.2) N (w) := { sα | α ∈ Φw } = {t ∈ T | ℓ(tw ) < ℓ (w)}.

If x, y ∈ W , we have (1.4.3) N (xy ) = N (x) + xN (y)x−1, Φxy = (Φ x \ (−xΦy )) ∪ (x(Φ y ) \ − Φx)where + denotes symmetric difference on the power set P(T ) of T (that is, A + B =(A ∪ B) \ (A ∩ B) = ( A \ B) ∪ (B \ A) for A, B ⊆ T ) . Further, (1.4.4) ℓ(x) = |N (x)| = |Φx|, if x ∈ W . 

1.5. The weak (right) order of W is the partial order ≤ on W defined by 

x ≤ y ⇐⇒ Φx ⊆ Φy ⇐⇒ N (x) ⊆ N (y) ⇐⇒ ℓ(y) = ℓ(x) + ℓ(x−1y),

for x, y ∈ W . One has 

x ≤ xy ⇐⇒ Φx−1 ∩ Φy = ∅ ⇐⇒ N (x−1) ∩ N (y) = ∅ ⇐⇒ ℓ(xy ) = ℓ(x) + ℓ(y).

The poset ( W, ≤) is a complete meet semilattice (see [3, Theorem 3.2.1]). 1.6. We say loosely that the pair (Φ , Π), with Π ⊆ Φ regarded as subsets of the real vector space V equipped with its bilinear form B and W -action, is a realized root system of ( W, S ). There may be uncountably many pairwise non-isomorphic (in the natural sense) realized root systems of a fixed (finite rank) Coxeter system (W, S ), even restricting to those for which Π is a basis of V .On the other hand, one may regard (Φ , Π) more abstractly as a pair consisting of a W × {± 1}-set Φ together with a subset Π of Φ and a specified bijection α 7 →

sα : Π → S. As such, it depends only on ( W, S ), up to the natural notion of isomorphism of such pairs. We shall call such a pair an abstract root system of (W, S ). Two well-known descriptions of the (unique up to isomorphism) abstract root system directly in terms of the Coxeter system ( W, S ) are recalled in 2.5–2.6. Many of the results discussed in 1.7–1.43 are of a purely algebraic and combina-torial nature, in that they can be formulated in terms of the abstract root system of ( W, S ), though we do not always do so when possible; however, currently known proofs of many of them require use of realized root systems. Later results of this section typically depend in an essential way on a choice of realized root system. 1.7. The subgroups WJ := ⟨ J ⟩ of W , for J ⊆ S, are called standard parabolic subgroups . Conjugates wW J w−1, where w ∈ W and J ⊆ S, of standard parabolic subgroups are called parabolic subgroups .In this and similar notation, we use indexing by sets of reflections, or by the corresponding sets of positive roots, interchangeably. For example, if Γ ⊆ Π, then 

WΓ := WJ where J := { sα | α ∈ Γ }. More generally, for any subset Γ of roots, we write WΓ = WR = ⟨ R ⟩ where R = { sα | α ∈ Γ }.ROOT SYSTEMS OF BRINK-HOWLETT GROUPOIDS 5

A subset J of Π, the corresponding subset { sα | α ∈ J } of simple reflections, or the subgroup WJ itself, is said to be spherical if WJ is finite. In that case, we denote the longest element of WJ as wJ ; it satisfies ( wJ )2 = 1, N (wJ ) = WJ ∩ T

and wJ J = −J.1.8. Brink-Howlett groupoids. Recall that a groupoid is a small category G in which every morphism is invertible. Such a groupoid is determined by its set Ob( G) of objects, the hom-sets Gb a := Hom G(a, b ) for a, b ∈ Ob( G) and composition maps (g, f ) 7 → gf : Gc b × Gb a → Gc a

subject to suitable (associativity, unit and inverse) conditions. A groupoid H is said to be connected if it has at least one object and there is a morphism a → b for any objects a and b of H. The inclusion-maximal connected subgroupoids H of G are called the connected components , or components , of G.By a representation of the groupoid G in a category C, we mean a functor 

F : G → C. In case C is a concrete category, given by a faithful “underlying set” functor U : C → Set (which is frequently suppressed from the notation) to the category of sets, we often abbreviate gx := ( U (F (g)))( x) ∈ U (F (b)) for g ∈ Gb a

and x ∈ U (F (a)). Then F is characterized by “action maps” (g, x ) 7 → gx : Gb a × U (F (a)) → U (F (b)) subject to suitable (associativity and unit) conditions. 1.9. In their study [9] of normalizers of standard parabolic subgroups, Brink and Howlett implicitly defined a groupoid bG as follows. The set of objects of bG is ob( bG) := P(Π), the power set of Π. For two subsets 

J and K of Π, a morphism J → K in bG is by definition a triple ( K, w, J ) where 

K = w(J); we may write such a morphism simply as w : J → K or even just w if J

and/or K are understood. Composition of morphisms is induced by multiplication in W : ( L, v, K )( K, w, J ) = ( L, vw, J ). We call bG the full Brink-Howlett groupoid of ( W, S ). Brink and Howlett mainly studied the connected components of bG. By a Brink-Howlett groupoid (of ( W, S )), we mean any groupoid G which is a union of components of bG.

Example 1.10 . Let J ⊆ Π and α ∈ Φ+ ∩ J⊥, where for any subset U of V ,

U ⊥ := { v ∈ V | B(v, u ) = 0 for all u ∈ U }

Then ( J, s α, J ) is a morphism in bG. Even for W of type B2, this morphism does not in general lie in the subgroupoid generated by morphisms ( J, s β , J ) with β ∈ Π ∩ α⊥.1.11. Brink and Howlett [9] showed that Brink-Howlett groupoids have several Coxeter group-like features, including an analogue of the presentation of Coxeter groups by simple generators subject to braid relations. They also showed that for any object J, a certain subgroup of the vertex group of morphisms J → J

(containing in particular all morphisms discussed in Example 1.10) is a Coxeter group with root system naturally constructed from a realized root system Φ of (W, S ). However, Brink and Howlett’s paper does not develop notions of root systems of Brink-Howlett groupoids. Similar Coxeter group-like groupoids also appear, with root systems, in the liter-ature as Coxeter (and Weyl) groupoids (see for instance [10] and [27]), although the Brink-Howlett groupoids of infinite Coxeter groups are not of that type in general. 6 M. DYER AND H. GIMENEZ 

Rudiments of a more general framework (rootoids, especially principal rootoids) including the Brink Howlett groupoids, and Coxeter and Weyl groupoids, are in-dicated in [15]–[16] and [19]. The main novelties in the theory of rootoids are its lattice- and category-theoretic underpinnings, and consequent closure of classes of rootoids under various natural category-theoretic constructions. The underlying groupoid of a principal rootoid admits a canonical presentation by generators and relations generalizing those of Coxeter groups, Coxeter groupoids and Brink-Howlett groupoids. A principal rootoid also admits a canonical analogue of the abstract root system of a Coxeter group, but, even if finite, does not in general have a “realized root system” in a real vector space, because of the existence of non-realizable oriented matroids (see [19]). Finite, complete, principal rootoids admit suitable “oriented matroid root systems” by [19], but it is an open question whether (or under what conditions) infinite principal rootoids do. Our first main goal in this paper is to interpret the standard abstract root system of a Brink-Howlett groupoid (which can be shown to be the standard root system of the corresponding principal rootoid, though we do not define this) directly in terms of the associated Coxeter system. We show that positive roots correspond to certain reflection subgroups, and the standard abstract root system may be nat-urally extended in various ways (e.g. allowing analogues of imaginary roots and non-reduced root systems) to abstract root systems with positive roots correspond-ing to larger and more natural classes of reflection subgroups. Our second main goal is to study realizations of these abstract root systems in real vector spaces. We obtain a satisfactory realization for some of these root systems, including the standard abstract root system, and those we consider with only real roots. Finally, we extend the definitions and some basic properties of Tits and imaginary cones of Coxeter groups to these realized root systems. 1.12. Signed groupoid-sets. We shall not discuss rootoids in this paper, but will make use of the very closely related notion of rootoidal signed groupoid-sets. These are not as well behaved category-theoretically as rootoids but are more concrete and better adapted for purposes here. Signed groupoid-sets are discussed in 1.13–1.20 and Section 3; for more detail and proofs, see [19]. For basic terminology and facts concerning ordered sets and lattices as used in this paper, see for example [11]. 1.13. The category Set ± of definitely signed sets has as its objects sets X with a specified free action of the sign group {± 1} = {±} and a distinguished set X+ of orbit representatives, called positive elements of X. Let X− := −X+ = { − x | x ∈

X+ }. The sign of x ∈ X is ϵ ∈ {±} where x ∈ Xϵ. Morphisms of definitely signed sets are {±} -equivariant maps, not necessarily preserving positive elements, with composition by composition of underlying functions. A signed groupoid-set is by definition a pair ( G, X ) where G is a groupoid and 

X : G → Set ± is a functor. We refer to ( G, X ), or just X for short, as a signed 

G-set . Since Set ± is a concrete category, we may (and usually do) describe signed 

G-sets by their action maps (g, x ) 7 → gx : Gb a × X(a) → X(b),

which in particular satisfy g(−x) = −gx . Here gx := ( X(g))( x). Elements of X(a), 

X(a)+ and 

X(a)− respectively are called roots , positive roots and negative roots of X (at 

a or based at a). We say a root in X(a) has a as its base (tacitly assuming in ROOT SYSTEMS OF BRINK-HOWLETT GROUPOIDS 7

doing so that the sets Xa := X(a) for a ∈ ob( G) are pairwise disjoint, as we do by convention unless otherwise stated). 1.14. There is a natural notion of morphism of signed groupoid-sets (see [15]–[16] but we shall not use it in this paper. However, the notion of a morphism of signed 

G-sets (for fixed G) defined below will be used extensively. Let ( G, X ) and ( G, Y ) be signed G-sets, where G is a groupoid. A morphism 

ν : ( G, X ) → (G, Y ) is defined to be a natural transformation ν : X → Y of functors 

G → Set ± for which the components νa : X(a) → Y (a) for a ∈ ob( G), which are morphisms of signed sets, are also positivity preserving in the sense they restrict to maps X(a)+ → Y (a)+. This gives rise to a category of signed G-sets, and thereby provides a natural notion of isomorphism of signed G-sets. We say the above morphism ν is an embedding of signed G-sets if each component 

νa with a ∈ ob( G) is injective as a map of sets. We then write ( G, X ) ,→ (G, Y ). We say that ( G, X ) is a signed G-subset of ( G, Y ) if there is an embedding 

ν : ( G, X ) ,→ (G, Y ) such that for each object a of G, the underlying function of the component νa : X(a) → Y (a) is an inclusion map (so in particular, X(a) ⊆ Y (a)). By abuse of notation, we then write ( G, X ) ⊆ (G, Y ). If there is an embedding (G, X ) ,→ (G, Y ), then ( G, X ) is isomorphic to a signed G-subset of ( G, Y ). 1.15. Let ( G, X ) be a signed groupoid-set. For any subgroupoid H of G, one has a signed groupoid-set ( H, X |H ), called the restriction of ( G, X ) to H, where the functor X|H : H → Set ± is the restriction of X : G → Set ±. The restriction of (G, X ) to a connected component H of G is called a (connected) component of (G, X ). 

Example 1.16 . Regard the Coxeter group W as a one-object groupoid W• with object • and W as the group of automorphisms of that object. Then the abstract root system Φ of ( W, S ) naturally gives rise to a signed W•-set ( W•, Φ•); here, Φ •

is the functor taking the object • of W• to the signed set Φ, and a morphism w of 

W• (i.e. an element w of W ) to the {±} -equivariant action by w on Φ. 1.17. Signed groupoid-sets are of greatest interest when they satisfy stringent ad-ditional conditions, discussed below, abstracted from those satisfied in example 1.16. See Section 3, [15] and [19] for more details. Let ( G, X ) be a signed groupoid-set. For an object a of G, the left star of a is the set Ga = ˙S 

> b∈ob( G)

Ga b of morphisms of G with codomain a. Here, we regard distinct hom-sets of a category as pairwise disjoint, as is a common convention. The left inversion set Xg of g ∈ Ga is the set (1.17.1) Xg := Xa + ∩ g( Xb −), g ∈ Ga b

of positive roots based at a which are made negative by g−1. Also define (1.17.2) bXg = Xa + + g( Xb +)where A + B := ( A \ B) ∪ (B \ A) = ( A ∪ B) \ (A ∩ B) denotes symmetric difference. Essentially by definition, the map g 7 → bXg defines a 1-coboundary for G, so we have the 1-cocycle property (1.17.3) bXgh = bXg + g( bXh),

Note also that for any morphism ρ : ( G, X ) → (G, Y ) of signed groupoid sets and any a ∈ ob( G) and g ∈ Ga , we have ρa(Xg ) ⊆ Yg , with equality for all a if 

ρa(X(a)) = Y (a) for all a.1.18. The weak (right) preorder of ( G, X ) based at a is the pre-order (that is, reflexive, transitive relation) ≤a on Ga defined by 

g ≤a h ⇐⇒ Xg ⊆ Xh, g, h ∈ Ga .

We say ( G, X ) is faithful if for any object a of G and any morphisms g, g ′ ∈ Ga ,one has Xg = Xg′ if and only if g = g′. Equivalently, ( G, X ) is faithful if and only if for each a ∈ ob( G), the weak preorder ( Ga , ≤a ) is a partial order, which is then called the weak order of ( G, X ) at a. One says that ( G, Λ) is interval finite if it is faithful and for any a ∈ ob( G), all closed intervals in the weak order ( Ga , ≤a ) are finite. We say that ( G, X ) is rootoidal if it is faithful and for each a ∈ Ob( G), (i) ( Ga , ≤a ) is a complete meet semilattice (i.e. any non-empty subset P of 

Ga has a meet V P ). (ii) if P ⊆ Ga and q ∈ Ga are such that Xp ∩ Xq = ∅ for each p ∈ P , and if the join p′ := W P in ( Ga , ≤a ) of P exists, then Xp′ ∩ Xq = ∅.The condition (ii) is called the Join Orthogonality Property (JOP) of ( Ga , ≤a ). It implies that ( Ga , ≤a ) embeds as a down-set in a complete ortholattice. 1.19. Rootoidal signed groupoid sets have many favorable properties, but addi-tional “discreteness” conditions are required in order to restrict to more “Coxeter-group-like” signed groupoid sets. For the following definitions, assume that ( G, X ) is a faithful signed groupoid-set. A morphism g ∈ Ga is called simple if |Xg | = 1 and atomic if g is an atom of the poset ( Ga , ≤a ). A simple morphism is clearly atomic. For any set of generators C of G, the length ℓC (g) of g is defined to be the minimal length of an expression of g as a product of elements of C and their inverses; by convention, ℓC (g) = 0 if g is an identity morphism. We say that ( G, X ) is principal if it is generated by its set R of simple morphisms, and for any morphism g of G, ℓR(g) = |Xg |.We say that ( G, X ) is preprincipal if it is generated by its set A of atomic morphisms and for any a ∈ Ob( G), g ∈ Ga and s ∈ Aa := Ga ∩ A, one has either 

Xs ⊆ Xg or Xs ∩ Xg = ∅.1.20. See Section 3 for more details of the following definitions and constructions. A root in Xa is said to be real if its sign is changed by the action of g−1 for some g ∈ Ga , and imaginary otherwise. These notions give rise to signed G-subsets (G, X re ) and ( G, X im ) of ( G, X ). Two roots α, β based at the same object a of G

are said to be parallel if g−1α and g−1β have the same sign as one another, for all 

g ∈ Ga .Some of the results involve two endofunctors of the category of signed G-sets, 

realification , denoted ( G, X ) → (G, X re ) on objects and ν 7 → νre on morphisms, and compression , denoted ( G, X ) → (G, X c) on objects and ν 7 → νc on morphisms. By definition, the positive (resp., negative) roots of Xre based at an object a of 

G are the positive (resp., negative) real roots of X which are based at a. The positive (resp., negative) roots of Xc based at an object a of G are the parallelism classes of positive (resp., negative) roots of X which are based at a. Realification ROOT SYSTEMS OF BRINK-HOWLETT GROUPOIDS 9

and compression commute and their composite, real compression , is denoted by (G, X ) 7 → (G, X rec ) on objects and ν 7 → νrec on morphisms. We say X is real if 

Xre ∼= X, compressed if Xc ∼= X and real compressed if Xrec ∼= X.Realification and compression don’t change the underlying groupoid of a signed groupoid set ( G, X ) or its weak preorders, so in particular, they preserve rootoidal-ity. If ( G, X ) is preprincipal, then ( G, X rec ) is real, compressed, principal, and the atomic generators of ( G, X ) are the simple generators of ( G, X rec ). The class of real, compressed, principal, rootoidal signed groupoid sets ( G, X ) has many features in common with the class of signed groupoid sets ( W•, Φ•) associated to Coxeter systems ( W, S ) (and also analogues for Brink-Howlett groupoids and Coxeter and Weyl groupoids). The class of preprincipal, rootoidal signed groupoid sets is an enlargement of that class which admits abstract analogues of non-reduced root sys-tems of finite Weyl groups and root systems (possibly with imaginary roots) of Coxeter groups of Kac-Moody Lie algebras. 1.21. Standard abstract root systems of Brink-Howlett groupoids. Let G denote a Brink-Howlett groupoid of ( W, S ) and ( W•, Φ•) denote the signed groupoid-set from Example 1.16. There is a functor F : G → W• such that F (J) = • for all 

J ∈ Ob( G) and F (J w

−→ K) = w. Composing the functor Φ • with F gives a signed groupoid-set (G, Λ) , Λ := Φ • ◦ F. 

The following is a main result of this paper, proved in Section 3. It is a conse-quence of more general facts, some discussed in [15]–[16], but we provide a direct proof. Note that part (ii) follows from (i), by the discussion in 1.20. 

Theorem 1.22. Define the signed groupoid-set (G, Λ) as in 1.21. We shall construct in this paper other signed G-sets with real compression isomorphic to ( G, Λrec ) as in Theorem 1.22. To begin, we recall the known explicit description of the Brink-Howlett generators, and related elements, of Brink-Howlett groupoids. 10 M. DYER AND H. GIMENEZ 

Let ( mr,s )r,s ∈S denote the Coxeter matrix of ( W, S ). By definition, mr,s := ord( rs ) ∈ N≥1 ∪ {∞} . Recall that the Coxeter graph is the undirected, edge-labelled (simple) graph with vertex set S and an edge joining r, s ∈ S if mr,s ≥ 3, that edge being labeled by mr,s if mr,s ≥ 4. Using the natural bijection α 7 → sα : Π → S, the Coxeter graph of ( W, S ) will often be regarded in this paper as an edge-labeled graph with vertex set Π. By a component of Π, we mean a connected component of this graph, or sometimes just the vertex set J of that component. We may then refer to { sα | α ∈ J } as a component of S, WJ as a component of W , Φ J as a component of Φ, Φ + 

> J

as a component of Φ +, etc. Similarly, we may refer to components of other Coxeter groups, their simple reflections, root systems, simple roots, and positive systems provided the sets of simple reflections and simple roots are clear from context (as they are for any reflection subgroup of ( W, S ), by conventions in 1.28). We refer to a component in any of these senses as being of finite, affine, indefinite, locally finite, infinite type etc according to the type of J (see [32] and [25]). The following facts, and the closely related Proposition 1.35, are essentially due to Howlett and Deodhar. See [29], [12], [9], and also [25] for W of possibly infinite rank. 

Lemma 1.25. Let J ⊆ K ⊆ Π. Then |(WK \ WJ ) ∩ T | is finite if and only if there are finitely many components of K which meet K \ J, and they are all of finite type. Assume that this is the case, and let L ⊇ K \ J be the union of all such components, which is a spherical subset of Π. Let w = ν(K \ J, J ) := wLwL∩J ∈ W . Then: (a) Φ+ 

> K

\ Φ+ 

> J

= Φ + 

> L

\ Φ+ 

> L∩J

= Φ w−1 .(b) (WK \ WJ ) ∩ T = ( WL \ WL∩J ) ∩ T = N (w−1).(c) wJ = ( J \ L) ∪ w(J ∩ L) = ( J \ L) ∪ − wL(J ∩ L) ⊆ K.(d) Writing wJ = J′ ⊆ K the element ν(K \ J′, J ′) of W is defined similarly as ν(K \ J, J ), and it satisfies ν(K \ J, J )−1 = ν(K \ J′, J ′) in W .(e) ν(K \ J, J ) = ν(L \ J, L ∩ J).Remark 1.26 . Whenever we use notation of the form ν(M, L ) in this paper, where 

M and L are subsets of the set of simple roots of a Coxeter system ( W, S ), it is tacitly assumed that M ∩ L = ∅ and |(WM ∪L \ WL) ∩ T | is finite, so ν(M, L ) is defined by the preceding lemma. 1.27. The triples ( J′, w, J ), with w = ν(K \ J, J ), defined as in Lemma 1.25, for which J (or equivalently, J′) are objects of a fixed Brink-Howlett groupoid G are morphisms of G. Those for which, additionally, K \ J (or equivalently, K \ J′)is a singleton set form a set of generators for G; we call them the Brink-Howlett generators of G. See [12], [9] and Proposition 3.13. We frequently conflate a singleton set {x} with x when convenient for notational compactness, so if K \ J =

{α}, may write ν(α, J ) := ν({α}, J ). In fact, G is generated by its Brink-Howlett generators [12], subject to relations of the following two types, called rank one relations and rank two relations in [9]. For any Brink-Howlett generator ( J′, ν (K \ J, J ), J ) of G as in Lemma 1.25(c), there is a rank one relation ( J′, ν (K \ J, J ), J )−1 = ( J, ν (K \ J′, J ′), J ′). There is a rank two relation for each morphism ( J′, w, J ) of G, with w = ν(K \

J, J ) where |K \ J| = 2 (or equivalently, |K \ J′| = 2). Such a morphism has exactly two expressions as a minimal length product of Brink-Howlett generators, one with 

ν(α, K ) as rightmost factor and the other with ν(β, K ) as rightmost factor, where ROOT SYSTEMS OF BRINK-HOWLETT GROUPOIDS 11 

K \ J = {α, β }; the corresponding relation expresses the equality of those two expressions. See [9]. 1.28. Maximal corank k overgroups of reflection subgroups. Roots of the signed groupoid-set in Theorem 1.22(b) will be seen to correspond to certain reflection subgroups of W . We describe below salient properties of reflection subgroups and their root subsystems. A reflection subgroup of W is by definition a subgroup U = ⟨ U ∩ T ⟩ of W which is generated by the reflections it contains. Let U be a reflection subgroup of W .Then U has a set of canonical Coxeter generators (depending on ( W, S )) (1.28.1) χ(U ) = χ(W,S )(U ) := { t ∈ T | N (t) ∩ u = {t} } ,

for which the set of reflections in U is U ∩ T . Further, ( U, χ (U )) has a based root system 1 (Φ U , ΠU ) with positive roots Φ + 

> U

in ( V, B ) where Φ U = { α ∈ Φ | sα ∈ U },Φ+ 

> U

:= Φ U ∩ Φ+ and Π U := { α ∈ Φ+ | sα ∈ χ(U ) }. In particular, if U = WJ is the standard parabolic subgroup generated by reflections in a subset J of simple roots, then Π U = J.Define the corank corank W (U ) of U in ( W, S ) to be the minimum cardinality of a subset T ′ ⊆ W ∩ T such that W = ⟨ (U ∩ T ) ∪ T ′ ⟩. For J ⊆ Π, one has in particular ΠWJ = J and corank W (wW J w−1) = |Π \ J| for any w ∈ W . See Proposition 2.11. We extend the definition of notions defined for Coxeter systems and their based root systems to arbitrary reflection subgroups U of ( W, S ) by regarding U as the Coxeter group underlying the Coxeter system ( U, χ (U )), for which one uses the based root system (Φ U , ΠU ). In particular, this defines corank U (U ′) for any reflec-tion subgroups U ′ and U of W with U ′ ⊆ U ; this corank is the minimum of the cardinalities of subsets T ′ of U ∩ T such that U = ⟨ (U ′ ∩ T ) ∪ T ′ ⟩.1.29. For any subgroup G of W , we say that a subgroup H of W with H ⊇ G is an overgroup of G (in W ). We say that H is an overgroup of G of index 2 [H : G](the index of G in H). If H and G are reflection subgroups of W , we say that H is a corank n reflection overgroup of G where n := corank H (G). If U is a reflection subgroup of W and WJ ⊆ U , then J ⊆ ΠU ; in that case, corank U (WJ ) = |ΠU \ J|. If the corank is equal to k, we say, according to the above, that U is a corank k reflection overgroup of WJ (in W ). For k ∈ N, a 

maximal corank k reflection overgroup of WJ is by definition an inclusion maximal element of the inclusion-ordered poset of corank k reflection overgroups of WJ .The following refinement of results in [17] (which concerned ranks of reflection subgroups) is important in this paper. It is proved and slightly refined in 2.32–2.42.

1.31. Roots and reflection subgroups. Note that reflections of ( W, S ) correspond bijectively to rank one reflection overgroups in W of the trivial group. Theorem 1.33 below generalizes to a Brink-Howlett groupoid G the description in 2.5 of the abstract root system of ( W, S ) in terms of reflections, and is a main result of this work. 1.34. For notational compactness, we may write ΥJ X := Υ X (J), and sometimes omit X if X = R, writing for instance Υ := Υ R and ΥJ := ΥJ R.We next discuss additional facts which are of interest in relation Theorem 1.33 or are involved in its proof. The first of these, as already mentioned, is essentially due to Howlett [29] and Deodhar [12]. It provides several equivalent descriptions of real roots in the root systems in Theorem 1.33. ROOT SYSTEMS OF BRINK-HOWLETT GROUPOIDS 13 

Proposition 1.35. Let J ⊆ Π, and U be a reflection overgroup of WJ , so J ⊆ ΠU .Then the following conditions are equivalent: (i) (U \ WJ ) ∩ T is finite (ii) ΦU \ ΦJ is finite. (iii) corank U (WJ ) = |ΠU \ J| is finite and for each α ∈ ΠU \ J, the component of ΠU containing α is of finite type. (iv) the index [U : WJ ] is finite. 

The next result follows immediately from Theorem 1.33 and Proposition 1.35. 

Corollary 1.36. The set Λrec (J)+ of positive real roots based at an object J of the standard signed groupoid-set (G, Λrec ) of a Brink-Howlett groupoid G is in natural bijective correspondence with the set of finite index, corank one, parabolic overgroups of WJ in W .

1.37. When the conditions in Proposition 1.35 hold, the element ν(Π U \ J, J ) ∈ U

is defined in the Coxeter system ( U, χ (U )). Natural examples of situations to which that proposition applies, with U standard parabolic in W , are given by the next proposition, which is proved as Proposition 2.29. 

Proposition 1.38. Let α ∈ Φ+. Fix K ⊆ Π with B(α, β ) ≥ 0 for all β ∈ K and let 

J := { β ∈ K | B(α, β ) = 0 }. Then [WK : WJ ] < ∞.

1.39. Special standard parabolic root subsystems. In the following, we regard Π as the vertex set of the Coxeter graph of ( W, S ). By a component of a subset J

of Π, we mean the vertex set of a component of the full subgraph the Coxeter graph on vertex set J. Let Jfin be the union of all finite type components of J and 

Jinf be the union of all other components (i.e. all finite components which are of affine or indefinite type, and all components which are infinite as sets) of J. Thus, 

J = Jfin ˙∪ Jinf .In order to study a connected Brink-Howlett groupoid of ( W, S ), Brink and Howlett replaced the groupoid by an isomorphic Brink-Howlett groupoid of a suit-able standard parabolic subgroup W ′ of W , to reduce to the case in which each object J satisfies Jinf = ∅ (that is, J = Jfin ), and so that no root in Π \ J is joined in the Coxeter graph of ( W, S ) to infinitely many elements of J. To obtain particu-larly well-behaved realizations in real vector spaces of root systems of Brink-Howlett groupoids, it is necessary to refine this reduction by restricting the W ′ action from 

V to a suitable subspace of V which contains Φ W ′ . This reduction and its effect on real root systems is described in Propositions 4.7 and 4.8. The proofs of those results uses Proposition 1.41, which is deduced from Proposition 1.38. 1.40. A standard parabolic root subsystem of Φ is by definition the root system ΦU of a standard parabolic subgroup U = WJ , for some J ⊆ Π. 

Proposition 1.41. Let L ⊆ Π.(a) If L = Linf , then Φ ∩ L⊥ is a standard parabolic root subsystem of Φ.(b) { β ∈ Φ | B(β, α )̸ = 0 for only finitely many α ∈ L } is a standard parabolic root subsystem of Φ.

1.42. Relative Tits theorem. The following theorem, proved in Section 2, is another of our main results. Its special case in which J = ∅ implies the theorem of Tits ([5]) that any finite subgroup of W is contained in a finite parabolic subgroup. 14 M. DYER AND H. GIMENEZ 

The special case when the corank is one in the final statement of the theorem is particularly important for our applications. 

Theorem 1.43. Let J ⊆ Π and W ′ be an overgroup in W of WJ . Then the following conditions are equivalent: (i) The index [W ′ : WJ ] of WJ in W ′ is finite. (ii) There is a parabolic subgroup U ⊇ W ′ of W such that [U : WJ ] is finite. (iii) There exist w ∈ W and K ⊆ Π so L := wJ ⊆ K, [WK : WL] is finite and 

WL = wW J w−1 ⊆ wW ′w−1 ⊆ WK .If (i)–(iii) hold, then there is a unique inclusion-minimal element U0 of the set of parabolic subgroups U which satisfy (ii). If, further, W ′ is a reflection subgroup, then corank U0 (WJ ) = corank W ′ (WJ ).

1.44. Realized root systems. By a realization of a signed groupoid-set ( G, Λ), we mean a pair ( V, ι ) consisting of a representation V of G in the category of real vector spaces together with a natural transformation ι : Λ → V (from the functor G → Set 

underlying Λ to that underlying V) such that the components ιa : Λ( a) → V (a) of 

ι are injective {±} -equivariant maps, for all a ∈ ob( G). The realizations of ( G, Λ) correspond naturally to families of realizations of its components. We shall often write the real vector space V(a) as Va .To emphasize the distinction between them, we call ( G, Λ) an abstract signed groupoid-set, and ( G, Λ, V, ι ) a realized signed groupoid-set. More informally, we call Λ an abstract root system of G and (Λ , V, ι ) a realized root system of G. This extends the terminology for root systems of Coxeter groups. 

Example 1.45 . Consider the signed W•-set ( W•, Φ•) in Example 1.16. There is a representation V• of W• in the category of real vector spaces, sending • to be ambient real vector space V of Φ, and each morphism w of W• to the linear map 

V → V induced by the action of w. There is a realization ( V•, ι ) of ( W•, Φ•) where 

ι : Φ • → V • is the natural transformation whose unique component ι• : Φ → V is the inclusion map. 1.46. Weak realizations. Define the notion of a weak realization (V, ι ) of a signed 

G-set ( G, Λ) in the same way as a realization, except omitting the requirement that the components ιa be injective maps. Several natural constructions yield in general only weak realizations of signed groupoid-sets, and one could extend the framework of this paper to deal with such situations more effectively by incorporating structure of “root multiplicity” into parts of the development (see 1.52). Consider a weak realization ( V, ι ) of ( G, Λ). Assume that for all a ∈ ob( G), one has (1.46.1) ιa(α)̸ = −ιa(β), α, β ∈ Λ( a)+.

Define the set eΛ( a) := ιa(Λ( a)) ⊆ V (a). This becomes a signed set with {±} 

action induced by that on V , and eΛ( a)+ := ιa(Λ( a)+) as the set of positive elements. The map ia : Λ( a) → eΛ( a) given by restriction of ιa is a morphism of signed sets. Let ja : eΛ( a) → V (a) denote the inclusion map, which is {±} -equivariant. For a morphism g : a → b in G, define eΛ( g) : eΛ( a) → eΛ( b) to be the map obtained by restriction of V(g) : V(a) → V (b). Then eΛ( g) is also a morphism of signed sets. This defines a functor eΛ : G → Set ±, making ( G, eΛ) a signed G-set. There is a natural transformation i : Λ → eΛ with component ia at a for each a ∈ ob( G). It ROOT SYSTEMS OF BRINK-HOWLETT GROUPOIDS 15 

gives a morphism i : ( G, Λ) → (G, eΛ) of signed G-sets, which is an isomorphism if (V, ι ) is a realization. There is a natural transformation j : eΛ → V (of set-valued functors) with com-ponent ja : eΛ( a) → V (a) at a ∈ ob( G). It is easily seen that ( G, eΛ, V, j ) is a realized signed groupoid set which we call the associated realized signed G-set of the weakly realized G-set ( G, Λ, V, ι ). 

Remark. The examples of (weak) realizations of signed groupoid-sets in which we are most interested satisfy convexity properties (to be studied in another work) which imply the condition (1.46.1). We do not impose corresponding conditions in the definition here, but simply observe that they hold in the examples considered. 1.47. Realized root systems of Brink-Howlett groupoids. We now describe con-struction of (weak) realizations of abstract root systems of Brink-Howlett groupoids considered in Theorem 1.33. The constructed realizations will generally depend, even up to the natural notion of isomorphism, not just on ( W, S ) but on the choice of a realized root system Φ of ( W, S ). We fix such a choice. Recall that for J ⊆ Π and any corank one reflection overgroup U ∈ RJ of WJ ,we define rU,J ∈ Φ+ \ J by (1.47.1) ΠU = J ˙∪{ rU,J }.

Our approach to constructing realizations of signed groupoid sets ( G, ΥX ) as in Theorem 1.33 is to represent an abstract root ( U, ϵ ) ∈ ΥX (J), where J ∈ ob( G), by ϵr U,J ∈ V . This element of V , together with J, determines ( U, ϵ ) uniquely by (1.47.1). However, Proposition 4.3 implies that this does not in general give a realization unless one replaces V by a suitable quotient vector space (depending on 

J in a manner compatible with the G-action) and replaces ϵr U,J by its image in the quotient. It is then possible that the images in the quotient space of rU,J and rU ′,J ,where U, U ′ ∈ XJ , could coincide even if U̸ = U ′. In general, one only obtains weak realizations in this way (see Example 6.12), but surprisingly (see Remark 1.51(2)), these induce a realization of the realification ( G, Υre

The other (i.e. not real) realized simple roots at J will be be called imaginary realized simple roots at J.Note that, especially in the case X is R or N , the realized root system eΥJ based at J may be non-reduced , in the sense that multiples cα , where c ∈ R>0, c̸ = 1, of realized (even simple) roots α may also be realized roots. See Proposition 6.14. 

Remark. In case F in Theorem 1.49 is only a weakly realized signed groupoid-set, it may be useful to regard each realized root α ∈ ιJ (Υ X (J))) as having an associated multiplicity, defined as the cardinal number |{ (U, ϵ ) ∈ ΥX (J) | ιJ (U, ϵ ) = α }| of its fiber for the map ιJ . Our results imply that real roots for G all have multiplicity equal to 1 in this sense (provided that one uses based root systems with all roots of unit square length). 1.53. Bilinear form. Let L ⊆ Π be such that all components of L are of finite type. Then the restriction of B to span( L) is positive definite and the subspaces span( L)and L⊥ of V are B-orthogonal with trivial intersection. The direct sum of these two subspaces consists of all elements v ∈ V such that B(v, α )̸ = 0 for only finitely many α ∈ L (see Section 6). We consider the following conditions on a subset J of Π. (i) For all α ∈ Π, we have B(α, β )̸ = 0 for only finitely many β ∈ Jfin .(ii) J = Jfin (or equivalently, Jinf = ∅). (iii) V = span( Jfin ) ⊕ (Jfin )⊥.From the preceding remarks, for any J ⊆ Π, (iii) implies (i). 1.54. Assume that 1.53(iii) holds for all objects J of G. In the weakly realized signed groupoid-set ( G, ΥX , V, ι ) of Theorem 1.49, we canonically identify, for each object J of G,(1.54.1) V(J) = V / span( Jfin ) = (( Jfin )⊥ ⊕ span( Jfin )) / span( Jfin ) = ( Jfin )⊥.

V(w : K → L) : ( Kfin )⊥ → (Lfin )⊥ is identified with the restriction of w : V → V .Denoting this map simply as w, it respects the bilinear forms, in the sense that 

BL(wv, wv ′) = BK (v, v ′), v, v ′ ∈ V (K),

since W acts by orthogonal transformations on ( V, B ).

1.59. Longest elements. The next proposition describes some aspects of an analogy between morphisms ν(K, J ) : J → J′ of G and longest elements of finite standard parabolic subgroups of Coxeter groups.

We say that a Brink-Howlett relation has domain J if it expresses the equality of two specified products of Brink-Howlett generators (and inverses), where both products have domain J. For fixed J, the sets K in the proposition such that K has cardinality one (resp., two) and ν(K, J ) exists are in natural bijective correspondence with the Brink-Howlett generators with domain J and also with the rank one Brink-Howlett relations with domain J (resp., with the rank two Brink-Howlett relations with domain J). 2.1. Throughout this paper, unless otherwise specified, ( W, S ) denotes a Coxeter system associated to a based root system (Φ , Π) in a real quadratic space ( V, B ). We may assume locally in the exposition or proofs that the Coxeter system or based root system satisfy additional conditions as convenience or necessity dictates, when there is no loss of generality in doing so. Two constructions which produce ROOT SYSTEMS OF BRINK-HOWLETT GROUPOIDS 21 

based root systems with useful additional properties are extension of the quadratic space, discussed in 2.2 and 2.3, and lifts, discussed in 2.33–2.34. 2.2. Extension of quadratic space. If ( V, B ) and ( V ′, B ′) are real quadratic spaces such that V is a subspace of V ′ and B is the restriction of B′ to a bilinear form on 

V , we say ( V, B ) is a restriction of ( V ′, B ′) and ( V ′, B ′) is an extension of ( V, B ). 3Extensions ( V, B ) of ( V, B ) may be constructed as follows. Let ( U, D ) be any real quadratic space, and C : U × V → R be any R-bilinear form. Take V ′ := V ⊕ U

and define the symmetric bilinear form B′ by 

B′(v + u, v ′ + u′) = B(v, v ′) + C(u, v ′) + C(u′, v ) + D(u, u ′),

for v, v ′ ∈ V and u, u ′ ∈ U .Extending the terminology of [25], a quadratic space ( V, B ) is said to be ample for a subset X of V if every linear map span( X) → R is of the form v 7 → B(v, v ′) : span(Π) → R for some v′ ∈ V . Then ( V, B ) is also ample for any subset X′ of span( X). Given a real quadratic space (V,B), one may choose an extension ( V ′, B ′) of (V, B ) as above such that ( V ′, B ′) is ample for V , and is thus ample for every subset of V . In fact, one may take U := V ⊕ Hom R(V, R) in the construction in the second paragraph of this subsection, any quadratic space ( U, D ) (for instance, 

D = 0) and C : U × V → R to be the evaluation pairing C(f, v ) := f (v). 2.3. Let ( V ′, B ′) be an extension of the quadratic space ( V, B ). Then any based root system (Φ , Π) in ( V, B ) is also a based root system in ( V ′, B ′), and the as-sociated Coxeter systems are canonically isomorphic (since they act faithfully on Φ ⊆ V ). Suppose given a based root system (Φ , Π) in the quadratic space ( V, B ). Then (V, B ) is ample for Π if and only if it is ample for Φ; in that case, we may say that (V, B ) is ample for (Φ , Π). 2.4. Abstract based root systems. There may be uncountably many choices of a realized root system Φ of a Coxeter system ( W, S ), up to isomorphism, even re-quiring the simple roots to be a basis of the ambient vector space V . However, the underlying structure of Φ as “abstract root system” (i.e. as a W × {± 1}-set with distinguished subsets of positive roots and simple roots) depends only on ( W, S ) as Coxeter system. In 2.5–2.6, we give two different (well-known) descriptions of this abstract root system directly in terms of ( W, S ). Many of the results discussed in this section before 1.39 are of a purely algebraic and combinatorial nature, in that they can be (but are not always, here) formulated in terms of the abstract root system of (W, S ); however, their currently known proofs often involve realized root systems. 2.5. We recall the definition (from [5] and [3], for instance) of an “abstract” ana-logue (Ξ , Θ) of (Φ , Π), which has the advantage it depends only on ( W, S ) (but the disadvantage that techniques from linear algebra, convex geometry etc which usefully apply to (Φ , Π) are not applicable to it). Let {± 1} be the cyclic group of order two with identity element +1. We some-times write it as {±} Define the W × {± 1}-set Ξ := T × {± 1} with {± 1} action by 22 M. DYER AND H. GIMENEZ 

multiplication on the right factor {± 1} of Ξ and with W -action defined by (2.5.1) w(t, ϵ ) = ( wtw −1, η w,t ϵ)for w ∈ W and t ∈ T , where η(w, t ) = −1 if t ∈ N (w−1) and η(w, t ) = +1 otherwise. This W -action is determined by that of the simple reflections, which is as follows: (2.5.2) s(t, ϵ ) =

We call the pair (Ξ , Θ) of a W × {±} -set Ξ with distinguished subset Θ the standard abstract based root system of ( W, S ). The relation of (Ξ , Θ) to the based root system (Φ , Π) is as follows: the map Φ → Ξ given by ϵα 7 → (sα, ϵ ) for α ∈ Φ+ and ϵ ∈ {± 1} is a W × {± 1}-equivariant bijection which maps Φ + to Ξ + and Π to Θ. 2.6. We recall another realization of the abstract root system of ( W, S ) which is used in the study of buildings (see [37], [1], [33]). The W -action ( w, x ) 7 → wx by left translation on W leads naturally to an action (w, A ) 7 → wA := { wa | a ∈ A } of W by left translation on the power set P(W ). Remark 2.7 . Note that as W -set, Ξ ′ is the union of the W -orbits on P(W ) of elements of Θ ′, the action by −1 is A 7 → W \ A, and the positive elements of Ξ ′ are those which contain 1 W . This description can be used to generalize the construction of (Ξ ′, Θ′) to preprincipal signed groupoid-sets, for which the atomic generators are good analogues of simple reflections. We do not know of any generalization of the construction in 2.5 to preprincipal signed groupoid sets, because of the absence in general of suitable analogues of conjugacy and reflections, but Theorem 1.33 provides such a generalization for Brink-Howlett groupoids, using the extra structure provided by the relationship of those groupoids to Coxeter systems. 2.8. Reflection subgroups. We record some basic facts concerning reflection sub-groups, shortest coset representatives, and corank. COS := { cos πn | n ∈ N≥2 } ∪ { λ ∈ R | λ ≥ 1 }.ROOT SYSTEMS OF BRINK-HOWLETT GROUPOIDS 23

2.12. The following proposition is included only to suggest the complexity of be-havior of ranks and coranks of general reflection subgroups of infinite Coxeter groups, which is similar (and related) to behavior of ranks of subgroups of non-abelian free groups. No subsequent use is made of the result. Recall that a Coxeter system is of locally finite type if each of its finite rank standard parabolic subgroups is finite. Write ℵ0 := |N|.

Proposition 2.13. Let (ni)i∈Z be a sequence such that n2i = 3 i + 3 for i ∈ N≥0,

n−2i ∈ N≥3 for i ∈ N>0 and n2i+1 = ℵ0 for i ∈ Z. Then if (W, S ) is irreducible and is not of finite, affine or locally finite type, it contains a chain (Wi)i∈Z of reflection subgroups such that Wi ⊊ Wj if i < j in Z and rank( Wi) = ni for all i ∈ Z.Proof. Recall that a Coxeter system ( U, R ) is universal if for any distinct r, s ∈ R,the order of rs is infinite. Firstly, from [25], the stated assumptions on ( W, S ) imply that W contains a universal reflection subgroup U of rank three. Secondly, in any rank three universal Coxeter group U such that χ(U ) = {r, s, t }, there is an infinite rank universal reflection subgroup U ′ such that χ(U ′) = { (rs )kt(rs )−k | k ∈ Z },which is denumerably infinite (see [20] or [13, Example 3.19]; note also that the subgroup ⟨ rs, rt ⟩ of W is free of rank two). One may construct a chain of reflection subgroups of ( W, S ) as described, all contained in U as in the first fact, by adroit use of the second fact. Details are left to the reader. □

2.14. Parabolic subgroups. The parabolic closure ParCl( G) of a subset (e.g. sub-group) G of W is defined to be the intersection of all parabolic subgroups of W

which contain G. In general, it is not necessarily a parabolic subgroup of W , but is a locally parabolic (reflection) subgroup as defined by Nuida [34]. We collect below some known facts about parabolic subgroups and parabolic closure. 

Proposition 2.15. Let U and U ′ be parabolic subgroups of W , and J ⊆ Π.(a) U ∩ U ′ is a parabolic subgroup of both U and U ′.(b) U ∩ U ′ is a parabolic subgroup of W .(c) If W ′ is a finite reflection subgroup of W , and P := ParCl( W ′), then P

is finite, rank( P ) = rank( W ′) = dim(span(Φ W ′ )) = |ΠW ′ | and ΦP =span(Φ W ′ ) ∩ Φ.(d) If W ′ = ⟨ J ∪ X ⟩ for some finite X ⊆ W , then ParCl( W ′) = P where 

P is the unique parabolic subgroup P ⊇ W ′ of minimum (finite) corank 

corank P (WJ ).ROOT SYSTEMS OF BRINK-HOWLETT GROUPOIDS 25 

Proof. Parts (a)-(c) are well-known; see for example [21, 2.4], [25, 2.7, 2.9, 2.12] and [33, 1.2.6, 3.2]. We prove (d). Since X is finite, there is a finite subset Q of Π such that X ⊆ WQ.For instance, take Q to be the set of all simple roots corresponding to simple reflections s ∈ S such that there is some x ∈ X such that s appears as a factor in some (equivalently, every) reduced expression of x. Let H := WJ∪Q. Clearly, 

W ′ ⊆ H. By definition of corank, corank H (WJ ) ≤ | Q| < ∞. Thus, H is a (standard) parabolic subgroup of W such that W ′ ⊆ H and corank H (WJ ) is finite. Amongst all parabolic subgroups P of W such that P ⊇ W ′, choose one for which corank P (WJ ) is minimal. Denote it as P , so corank P (WJ ) ≤ corank H (WJ ) < ∞.Suppose that P ′ is a parabolic subgroup of W such that W ′ ⊆ P ′. Note that 

P ′ ∩ P is parabolic in P by (b), and P ′ ∩ P ⊇ W ′ ⊇ WJ . Since WJ is standard parabolic in W , it is (standard) parabolic in any reflection subgroup (such as P ′ ∩P )which contains it. By Proposition 2.11(d), corank P (P ′ ∩ P ) + corank P ′∩P (WJ ) = corank P (WJ ) < ∞.

By choice of P , corank P ′∩P (WJ ) ≥ corank P (WJ ). This forces corank P ′∩P (WJ ) = corank P (WJ ) and corank P (P ′ ∩ P ) = 0 and so P = P ′ ∩ P by Proposition 2.11(a). Thus, P ⊆ P ′. Hence P is the inclusion-minimum parabolic subgroup containing 

W ′, which implies ParCl( W ′) = P . This proves (d). □

dJ ⊆ K.Proof. This holds since dΠW ′ = K, by Proposition 2.10(d), and J ⊆ ΠW ′ . □

We shall also require some elementary convex geometry. Given a cone C =cone( X) in V , a subset F of C is called a face of C if F = cone( F ) is itself a cone, and for all x, y ∈ C, if x + y ∈ F then x, y ∈ F . For any f ∈ V ∗ := Hom( V, R) such that f (C) ⊆ R≥0, C ∩ ker f is a face of C, called an exposed face . We use below the well-known fact that all faces of a polyhedral cone in V (i.e. a cone of the form cone( X) where X ⊆ V is finite) are exposed faces (see for instance [39]).

2.22. We digress to observe an obvious consequence of the preceding proposition which was not explicitly highlighted in [38] (where results essentially equivalent to some of those above were obtained in finite rank) or [25]. Define the presentation graph of a Coxeter system ( W, S ) to be the edge-labeled (undirected, simple) graph with vertex set S and an edge {r, s } joining r, s ∈ S if 

r̸ = s and rs has finite order mr,s < ∞, that edge being labeled by the positive integer mr,s if mr,s > 2. The presentation graph should not be confused with the 28 M. DYER AND H. GIMENEZ 

Coxeter graph, which may have edges labeled by ∞ and does not have edges joining commuting simple reflections. The definition of based root system implies that the map α 7 → cone( α) is a bijection between Π and the set of one-dimensional faces (i.e. extreme rays) of cone(Π). The following result, which is an immediate consequence of Proposition 2.21(b) shows that the edges of the presentation graph correspond to certain (not necessarily all) two-dimensional faces of cone(Π). 

Corollary 2.23. Let α, β ∈ Π be distinct. If there is an edge of the presentation graph joining sα and sβ , then cone( {α, β }) is a face of cone(Π) .Remark. Parts of Proposition 2.21 can be strengthened and generalized, leading in particular to more general constructions of realized or weakly realized root systems of Brink-Howlett groupoids based on the more general root systems of Coxeter groups described in [26], and extensions of Corollary 2.23 and parts of Proposition 2.21 to Brink-Howlett groupoids. We do not discuss this further in this paper. 2.24. The corollary above can be reformulated as follows if ( W, S ) has finite rank. Assume that Π is finite, so cone(Π) is a polyhedral cone. One may choose an affine hyperplane H in V such that for each α ∈ Π, cone( α)∩H = {pα} is a singleton set with pα̸ = 0. Then P := H ∩ cone(Π) is a convex polytope (a polytopal cross-section of cone(Π)) and the map α 7 → pα induces a bijection from Π to the set of vertices of P . In this situation, the corollary implies that if α, β ∈ Π are distinct and there is an edge of the presentation graph joining sα and sβ , then there is an edge of P with pα and pβ as its vertices. Hence (the unlabelled graph underlying) the presentation graph identifies with a subgraph (containing all vertices) of the edge-graph of the convex polytope P . More generally, the abstract simplicial complex of spherical subsets of Π has a geometric realization which is a subcomplex of the geometric simplicial complex of simplicial faces of P . See Example 6.12. 2.25. Finiteness criteria. We collect below some variants, which are useful in this paper, of known finiteness criteria for irreducible Coxeter systems. (There are many other important finiteness criteria we do not list.) Some of the cited proofs involve reduction of the results in general to their special cases for finite rank Coxeter systems in [12, §4] (see also [33, Proposition 1.2.6]). 

Proposition 2.26. Assume that (W, S ) is irreducible (so S̸ = ∅). Then the following conditions are equivalent. (i) W is finite. (ii) Φ is finite. (iii) For some J ⊊ Π, W J is finite. (iv) For some J ⊊ Π, Φ \ ΦJ is finite. (v) For some α ∈ Π, the W -orbit W α on Φ is finite. (vi) For some J ⊊ Π, the index [W : WJ ] is finite. (vii) For some J ⊊ Π, (W \ WJ ) ∩ T is finite. (viii) For some s ∈ S, the conjugacy class in W of s is finite. In conditions (iii)–(viii), “some” can be replaced by “all”. Proof. It is well known that (i) and (ii) are equivalent. Indeed, we have (ii) = ⇒ (i) since the natural W -action on Φ is faithful. The converse implication (i) = ⇒ (ii). holds since the map β 7 → sβ : Φ → W has the {±} -orbits on Φ as its non-empty ROOT SYSTEMS OF BRINK-HOWLETT GROUPOIDS 29 

fibers. This map β 7 → sβ restricts to surjections Φ \ ΦJ ↠ (W \ WJ ) ∩ T for J ⊆ Π, and W α ↠ { ws αw−1 | w ∈ W } for α ∈ Π, from which it follows that (iv) ⇐⇒ (vii) and (v) ⇐⇒ (viii). We also have (iii) ⇐⇒ (vi) since for J ⊆ Π, W J is a set of left coset representatives for WJ in W .By the equivalence of (i) and (ii), condition (i) implies the variants of (iii)-(viii) with “all” instead of “some.” Also, each such variant with “all” implies the corresponding condition with “some,” since S and Π are non-empty. Finally, by [25, Proposition 1.21 and Lemma 1.23], each condition (iii), (iv) or (v) implies (i). □

2.27. Root poset. Define a relation ⋖ on Φ such that ρ ⋖ τ if ρ, τ ∈ Φ and there exists α ∈ Π such that ρ = sατ and 0 ̸ = τ − ρ ∈ cone( α). Let ≤ denote the preorder on Φ which is defined as the reflexive, transitive closure of the relation ⋖. We call (Φ , ≤) the root poset of (Φ , Π), as justified by the following proposition. This differs slightly from [3, Chapter 4], where the restriction of ≤ to a partial order on Φ + is called the root poset. 

Proposition 2.28. (a) The preorder ≤ on Φ is a partial order. (b) The map ρ 7 → − ρ is an antitone (i.e. order reversing) bijection of the root poset with itself. (c) Define a function ℓ′ : Φ → Z by ℓ′(ϵα ) = ϵℓ (sα) for α ∈ Φ+ and ϵ ∈ {± 1}.Then ℓ′(τ ) is odd and ℓ′(−τ ) = −ℓ′(τ ) for all τ ∈ Φ.(d) If ρ, τ ∈ Φ with ρ ⋖ τ , then ℓ′(τ ) = ℓ′(ρ) + 2 .(e) ⋖ is the covering relation for (Φ , ≤).(f ) If τ ∈ Φ+, then {ρ ∈ Φ+ | ρ ≤ τ } is finite. (g) For any ρ ≤ τ in Φ, the closed interval [ρ, τ ] := { σ ∈ Φ | ρ ≤ σ ≤ τ } in the root poset is finite. Proof. For (a), we have to show ≤ is anti-symmetric. The definitions of ⋖ and ≤

imply that if ρ ≤ τ in Φ, then τ − ρ ∈ cone(Π). Since cone(Π) ∩ − cone(Π) = {0},(a) follows. Part (b) follows from the easily checked fact that if ρ ⋖ τ in Φ, then 

−τ ⋖ −ρ. Part (c) s trivial. We prove (d). Let ρ, τ ∈ Φ with ρ ⋖ τ . If ρ and τ are both positive roots, or both negative roots, the result follows from the definitions using the notion of the 30 M. DYER AND H. GIMENEZ 

depth of a positive root and its relation to length of the corresponding reflection (see [23, Equation (1) and Proposition 2.4]). Otherwise, we have τ ∈ Φ+ and 

ρ = sα(τ ) ∈ Φ− for some α ∈ Π, which implies τ = α = −ρ ∈ Π, ℓ′(τ ) = 1 and so 

ℓ′(ρ) = −1. Part (e) follows easily from (d) and the definitions. We prove (f). Fix τ ∈ Φ+. Suppose that ρ ⋖ τ in Φ with ρ ∈ Φ+. Let α ∈ Πwith ρ = sατ and 0 ̸ = τ − ρ ∈ cone( α). Then sτ = sαsρsα with ℓ(sτ ) = ℓ(sρ) + 2 by (d). Hence sρ ≤′ sτ where ≤′ is Bruhat order of ( W, S ). It now follows that for any τ ∈ Φ+, any ρ ∈ Φ+ with ρ ≤ τ satisfies 1 ≤′ sρ ≤′ sτ . Since Bruhat intervals are finite and β 7 → sβ : Φ + → T is injective, (f) follows. Finally, (g) follows from (b)–(c) on noting that [ρ, τ ] ⊆ { σ ∈ Φ+ | σ ≤ τ } ∪ { σ ∈ Φ− | ρ ≤ σ }. □

2.32. Maximal corank k reflection overgroups. We next prove Theorem 1.30, which generalizes facts about rank k reflection subgroups in [17]. For convenience, sub-sections 2.33–2.38 recall without proof the definitions and technical facts from [17] concerning “lifts” of root systems, which are used in the proofs here. 2.33. The category C of lifts of root systems has as objects the quadruples D =(V0, B 0, Φ0, Π0) where (Φ 0, Π0) is a based root system for the real quadratic space (V0, B 0). A morphism L : D1 → D2 in C, where Di = ( Vi, B i, Φi, Πi), is by defi-nition a morphism L : ( V1, B 1) → (V2, B 2) of quadratic spaces (that is, a R-linear map L : V1 → V2 which satisfies B2(L(u), L (v)) = B1(u, v ) for all u and v in V1)such that the function L : V1 → V2 restricts to a bijection Π 1 → Π2. Composition of morphisms is given by composition of the underlying linear maps. We call D1

above (or more precisely, the pair ( D1, L )) a lift of D2.Consider a morphism L : D1 → D2 in C as above. Let ( Wi, S i) and Φ + 

> i

denote respectively the Coxeter system and positive roots attached to Di, for i = 1 , 2. The map defined by sα 7 → sL(α) : S1 → S2, for α ∈ Π1, is a bijection which extends uniquely to a group isomorphism θ : W1 → W2. That is, θ : ( W1, S 1) →

(W2, S 2) is an isomorphism of Coxeter systems. Unless otherwise specified, we identify ( W2, S 2) = ( W1, S 1) by means of θ, so θ = Id W1 . One then has L(w(u)) = 

w(L(u)) for all w ∈ W1 and u ∈ V1. Further, L restricts to bijections Φ 1 → Φ2 and Φ+1 → Φ+2 satisfying sL(α) = sα for all α ∈ Φ1.2.34. For any fixed object D = ( V, B, Φ, Π) of C, there exists an object eD =( eV , eB, eΦ, eΠ) of C and a morphism L : eD → D such that eΠ is a R-basis of eV . The pair ( eD, L ) is uniquely determined by D up to isomorphism as an object of the category of objects of C over D; we call eD (with L understood), the universal lift of D, and call eΦ a universal lift of Φ. A universal lift of a lift of D is a universal lift of D.2.35. In the following, consider a based root system (Φ , Π), with associated Cox-eter system ( W, S ), in a quadratic space ( V, B ). Note that D = ( V, B, Φ, Π) is an object of the category C. For any reflection subgroup W ′ of W , there is an object 

DW ′ := ( V, B, ΦW ′ , ΠW ′ ) of C.Consider a lift ( D′, L ) of DW ′ where D′ = ( V ′, B ′, Ψ, ∆). A reflection subgroup

Hence dim span(Π W ′ )/ span( J) > k . On the other hand, 

k + 1 = corank W ′ (WJ ) = |ΠW ′ \ J| ≥ dim(span(Π W ′ )/ span( J)) > k. 

It follows that dim(span(Π W ′ )/ span( J)) = |ΠW ′ \ J| = k + 1 ∈ N.

Now since J ⊆ ΠW ′ is linearly independent, it follows that Π W ′ is linearly indepen-dent as claimed. From the two claims above, the definition of I and Proposition 2.39(b), it follows directly that for each α ∈ Φ \ ΦU , there is a unique element W ′ ∈ I such that 

⟨U ∪ { sα}⟩ ⊆ W ′, or equivalently by definition of I, such that α ∈ ΦW ′ . This is what we were required to prove. □

Proof of Theorem 1.30. Theorem 1.30(b)–(c) follow from Theorem 2.40(a)–(b) re-spectively. To prove Theorem 1.30(a), there is no loss of generality in assuming that Π is linearly independent, by taking a universal lift of Φ, for instance. Then Φ U

is linearly independent and Φ U = Φ ∩ span(Φ U ) by Proposition 2.21, so Theorem 1.30(a) follows from Theorem 2.40(b). □

2.41. The following proposition constructs maximal rank k reflection overgroups under purely group-theoretic assumptions which are more general than the condi-tions in Theorem 1.30(b). However, the resulting maximal rank k reflection over-groups are not known to be unique, as in Theorem 1.30(b). 

Proposition 2.42. Assume that J ⊆ Π and that U is a reflection overgroup of WJ

such that corank U (WJ ) = k ∈ N. Assume also that there is no reflection overgroup 

U ′ of U with corank U ′ (WJ ) < k (this holds for instance if ΠU is linearly independent or if k = 1 ). Then there are only finitely many reflection overgroups U ′ of U such that corank U ′ (WJ ) = k. In particular, U is contained in some inclusion maximal corank k reflection overgroup of WJ

Proof. Let U be any subgroup of W . Let QU denote the set of all reflection sub-groups V of W such that V contains U and U is not contained in any proper standard parabolic subgroup ⟨ K ⟩, where K ⊊ χ(V ), of V . An argument similar to that in the proof of [21, Proposition 1] shows that if U = ⟨ WJ ∪ X ⟩ for some finite subset X of W , then QU is finite. Now suppose that U is a reflection overgroup of 

WJ satisfying the assumptions of the proposition. Then U = ⟨ WJ ∪ X ⟩ for some finite subset X of T , so QU is finite. We claim that any reflection overgroup U ′ of 

U such that corank U ′ (WJ ) = k is an element of QU . For otherwise, U ′ would have a proper standard parabolic subgroup U ′′ , where Π U ′′ ⊊ ΠU ′ , such that U ⊆ U ′′ 

and we would have 

k = corank U ′ (WJ ) = |ΠU ′ \J| = |ΠU ′ \ΠU ′′ |+|ΠU ′′ \J| > |ΠU ′′ \J| = corank U ′′ (WJ ),

contrary to the assumptions. □38 M. DYER AND H. GIMENEZ

Proposition 2.44. (a) Suppose that α, β and γ are in Φ and γ − β ∈ span( α).Then either γ = β or γ = sα(β). 2.45. We call a linear map ht as in (a) in the following proposition a height function 

on V for (Φ , Π). 

Proposition 2.46. Assume that (W, S ) is of finite rank. (a) There exists a linear function ht : V → R such that ht( α) > 0 for all α ∈ Π.(b) For any function ht as in (a), there exists a positive constant ϵ such that 

ht( α) > ϵℓ (sα) for all α ∈ Φ+.ROOT SYSTEMS OF BRINK-HOWLETT GROUPOIDS 39 

Proof. We sketch a proof. Part (a) follows from basic facts about duals of polyhedral cones; see [25, Lemma 4.2(d)] for an equivalent result. Part (b) holds on taking 

ϵ := ϵ1ϵ2 where ϵ1 > 0 is chosen so that 0 < ϵ 1 < ht( α) for all α ∈ Π, and ϵ2 is chosen so 0 < ϵ 2 < 1 and ϵ2 < |B(α, β )| and for all pairs ( α, β ) of non-orthogonal simple roots such that there is a (rank two) finite standard parabolic root subsystem of Φ containing both α and β. (This implies that ϵ2 < |B(α, β )| for any non-orthogonal roots α and β; see [14, Lemma 1.22(1)].) The above more precise version of (b) can be proved by induction on ℓ(sα), in a similar manner to the proof of its special case [14, Lemma 1.22(2)]. □

3. Brink-Howlett groupoids as signed groupoid-sets 

Subsections 3.1–3.9 provide more details concerning general signed groupoid sets, and especially the notions of real and imaginary roots, realification and compression. The rest of Section 3 provides a proof of Theorem 1.22. 3.1. Let ( G, X ) be a signed G-set. For any a ∈ ob( G), each root x ∈ X(a) has a sign sgn( x) ∈ {±} = {± 1}, with sgn( x) = + if x ∈ X(a)+ or sgn( x) = −

if x ∈ X(a)−. We say that x is an imaginary root if sgn( x) = sgn( gx ) for all

Using imaginary roots instead of real roots, there is a similarly defined endo-functor, denoted ( G, X ) 7 → (G, X im ) on objects, and ν 7 → νim on morphisms, of the category of signed G-sets. positive imaginary roots and negative imaginary roots of X based at a. We note however that ( G, X ) ∼= ( G, X re ) `(G, X im ) where ` denotes coproduct in the cat-egory of signed G-sets. 3.3. For each object a of G, the dominance preorder 5 ⪯a of ( G, X ) at a is the preorder on X(a) which is defined as follows: for x, y ∈ X(a), let x ⪯a y if for all 

b ∈ ob( G) and g ∈ Gb a, we have gy ∈ X−(b) =⇒ gx ∈ X−(b). There is then an associated equivalence relation ∼a on X(a), which we call parallelism (at a), in which 

x ∼a y ⇐⇒ x ⪯a y and y ⪯a x, x, y ∈ X(a).

All roots in the parallelism class [x]a := { y ∈ X(a) | y ∼a x } (i.e. ∼a -equivalence class) of a root x based at a have the sign as x, and we define the sign (positive or negative) of [x]a to be equal to that of x.Note that if a root is real, then so is every root in its parallelism class. The parallelism class of an imaginary root is the set of all imaginary roots with the same base and sign as that root. We say that ( G, X ) is compressed if all of the dominance preorders ( Xa , ⪯a )with a ∈ ob( G) are partial orders, in which case we call them dominance orders .Note that ( G, X ) is compressed if and only if any two parallel roots of X (which necessarily have the same base) are equal. 3.4. Let Xc(a) denote the set of all parallelism classes in Xa . That is, 

Xc(a) := X(a)/ ∼a = { [x]a | x ∈ Xa }.

This is a signed set with − [a x] := [a −x] and positive and negative elements as previously defined: Xc,ϵ (a) = { [x]a | x ∈ Xϵ(a) } for ϵ ∈ {±} .The compression of ( G, X ) is defined to be the signed G-set ( G, X c) where Xc,as a functor from G to the category of signed sets, has value Xc(a) at each object 

a of G equal to the signed set just defined, and is given on morphisms of G by 

g [a x] = ( Xc(g))( [ a x]) := [gx ]b , for g ∈ Gb a and x ∈ X(a). It follows that ( G, X ) is compressed if and only if it is isomorphic to ( G, X c), and (G, (Xc)c) ∼= ( G, X c). Proposition 3.7. Let (G, X ) be a signed groupoid set. (a) For a ∈ ob( G), the weak preorders ( Ga , ≤a ) of (G, X ), (G, X re ), (G, X c)

and (G, X rec ) at a are equal. (b) If any of the four signed G-sets in (a) is faithful (resp., preprincipal), then they all are. Proof. Using (3.1.1), the weak preorders at a of ( G, X ) and ( G, X re ) are equal, and, by the definitions, ( G, X ) is faithful (resp., preprincipal) if and only if ( G, X re ) is faithful (resp., preprincipal). Similarly, using (3.4.1), the weak preorders of ( G, X )and ( G, X c) are equal, and by the definitions, ( G, X ) is faithful (resp., preprincipal) if and only if ( G, X c) is faithful (resp., preprincipal). All the assertions now follow on recalling that ( G, (Xre )c) = ( G, X rec ). □

We shall also use the following general facts (see [19] or [15]–[16]). 

Proposition 3.8. Let (G, X ) is preprincipal signed groupoid set, and let A denote the set of atomic generators of (G, X ).42 M. DYER AND H. GIMENEZ 

(a) For any a ∈ ob( G), the set of parallelism classes of real roots of (G, X ) at

Example 3.9 . The signed W -set ( W•, Φ•) of Example 1.16 is real, compressed, principal and rootoidal. If W is, further, a finite Weyl group, it has also a (possibly non-reduced) crys-tallographic root system Ψ in the sense of [5]; this has a set of simple roots ∆, but (Ψ , ∆) is not a based root system in the sense of this paper. The natural W -action on Ψ gives rise, in a similar manner to the construction of ( W•, Φ•), to a signed groupoid set ( W•, Ψ•). Each parallelism classes of roots for ( W•, Ψ•) is either a singleton set {α} or of the form {α, 2α} for some root α ∈ Ψ. The set of parallelism classes is in bijection with the set of indivisible roots α of Φ. Thus, ( W•, Φ•) is compressed if and only if Ψ is reduced in the usual sense (that is, α ∈ Ψ implies 2α̸ ∈ Ψ); in that case, ( W•, Φ•) and ( W•, Ψ•) are isomorphic as signed W•-sets. It follows also that ( W•, Ψ•) is principal if and only if Ψ is reduced. Even if Ψ is non-reduced, ( W•, Ψ•) is real, preprincipal and rootoidal, and its (real) compression is isomorphic to ( W•, Φ•). 3.10. Abstract root systems of Brink-Howlett groupoid sets. In the remainder of this section, ( G, Λ) denotes the signed groupoid set attached to a Brink-Howlett groupoid G associated to the Coxeter system ( W, S ) and its based root system (Φ , Π) as in 1.21. Recall that the weak order of ( W, S ) is denoted as ( W, ≤). 

Proposition 3.11. Let J ∈ ob( G) and g, g ′ ∈ GJ . Write g = ( J, w, K ) and g′ =(J, w ′, K ) where K, K ′ ⊆ Π, w, w ′ ∈ W and wK = w′K′ = J.(a) |Λg | is finite. (b) g ≤J g′ if and only if w ≤ w′.(c) If Λg = Λ g′ , then g = g′. That is, (G, Λ) is faithful. (d) The map FJ : ( GJ , ≤J ) → (W, ≤), induced by restriction to GJ of the map of morphisms for the functor F : G → W• in 1.21, is an order-embedding. (e) For each object K of G, each closed interval in the poset ( GK , ≤K ) is finite. That is, (G, Λ) is interval finite. Proof. We have Λ( J)+ = Φ +and Λ( J)− = Φ −, so (3.11.1) Λg = Λ( J)+ ∩ w(Λ( K)−) = Φ + ∩ w(Φ −) = Φ w.

Part (a) follows since |Φw| = ℓ(w) < ∞. The definitions imply from above that (3.11.2) g ≤J g′ ⇐⇒ Λg ⊆ Λg′ ⇐⇒ Φw ⊆ Φw′ ⇐⇒ w ≤ w′,

proving (b). If Λ g = Λ g′ , then from above, g ≤J g′ and g′ ≤J g and hence w ≤ w′

and w′ ≤ w, so w = w′. Then K = w−1J = w′− 1J = K′ so g = g′, proving (c). In (d), we have FJ (( J, w, K )) = w for ( J, w, K ) ∈ GJ . Therefore, FJ is an order embedding by (b). Part (e) follows from (a), (c) and the definition of the weak order ≤J , or from (d) and the fact intervals in weak order on W are finite. □

3.15. We now discuss squares in the signed groupoid set R = ( W•, Φ•), stating only the minimum needed for proofs in this section. For more extensive discussions of squares in various levels of generality, see [16] and [19]. In the following, we regard any subset of W as a poset in the order induced by weak order ≤ on W .

Definition 3.16. A square (of W ) is a quadruple ( w, x, y, z ) of elements of W such that wx = yz and w(Φ x) = Φ y :

Proposition 3.17. Let x, y, z and w be elements of W . Then the following conditions are equivalent: (i) (w, x, y, z ) is a square. That is, wx = yz and w(Φ x) = Φ y .(ii) wx = yz and Φwx = Φ w ˙∪ Φy .(iii) (y, z, w, x ) is a square. That is, yz = wx and y(Φ z ) = Φ w.(iv) (w−1, y, x, z −1) is a square. That is, w−1y = xz −1 and w−1(Φ y ) = Φ x.(v) wx = yz and in weak order on W , w ∨ y = wx and w−1 ∨ x = w−1y.(vi) wx = yz and in weak order on W , w ∨ y ≤ wx and w−1 ∨ x ≤ w−1y.Remarks. (1) There is an action of the dihedral group of order 8 on W 4, in which the two simple reflections act by the maps ( w, x, y, z ) 7 → (y, z, w, x ) and ( w, x, y, z ) 7 →

(w−1, y, x, z −1). Since (i) ⇐⇒ (iii) ⇐⇒ (iv), the set of squares of W is invariant under this action. (2) By (1), the dihedral group of order 8 also acts naturally on the “characteri-zations” (of which (i)–(vi) provide examples) of squares ( w, x, y, z ). (3) A square ( w, x, y, z ) is uniquely determined by the ordered pair ( w, x ) (resp., (w, y ), ( y, z ), ( x, z )). 

Proof. We show (i) = ⇒ (ii). Suppose that (i) holds. Since w(Φ x) = Φ y ⊆ Φ+, we have Φ x ∩ Φw−1 = ∅ and hence Φ wx = Φ w ˙∪ w(Φ x) = Φ w ˙∪ Φy , which implies (ii). We prove (ii) = ⇒ (i). Assume (ii) holds. Since Φ w ⊆ Φwx , we have w ≤ wx and so Φ wx = Φ w ˙∪ w(Φ x). By (ii). we get Φ y = w(Φ x), proving (i). Condition (ii) is equivalent to yz = wx and Φ yz = Φ y ˙∪ Φw, which by symmetry and (ii) ⇐⇒ (i), is equivalent to (iii). Condition (i) is equivalent to y−1w = zx −1 and w−1(Φ y ) = Φ x, which is (iv). Hence (i) ⇐⇒ (iv). We show (i) = ⇒ (v). Suppose (i) holds. Then (ii) holds, and it implies w ∨ y =

wx . Similarly, since (iv) holds, we have w−1 ∨ x = w−1y. Hence (v) holds. ROOT SYSTEMS OF BRINK-HOWLETT GROUPOIDS 45 

It is trivial that (v) implies (vi). Finally, we prove that (vi) = ⇒ (i). Suppose (vi) holds. Since w ≤ w ∨ y ≤ wx , we have Φ w−1 ∩ Φx = ∅. Similarly, w−1 ≤ w−1 ∨ x ≤

w−1y implies Φ w ∩ Φy = ∅. These imply Φw ˙∪ Φy ⊆ Φw∨y ⊆ Φwx = Φ w ˙∪ w(Φ x)and so Φ y ⊆ w(Φ x). Similarly, Φw−1 ˙∪ Φx ⊆ Φw−1∨x ⊆ Φw−1y = Φ w−1 ˙∪ w−1(Φ y )and thus Φ x ⊆ w−1(Φ y ) i.e. w(Φ x) ⊆ Φy . So we have wx = yz and w(Φ x) = Φ y ,proving (i). □

3.18. Recall that a complete meet semi-lattice is a non-empty poset ( X, ⪯) such that every non-empty subset of X has a meet (also called its glb or inf) in X.Let X be a complete meet semilattice. Then any subset A of X which has an upper bound in X has a join (also called its lub or sup) in X; that join is the meet of the set of upper bounds of A. We say that a non-empty subset Y of X is a 

join-closed meet sub-semilattice of X if for any non-empty subset A of Y , its meet V A in X satisfies V A ∈ Y , and further, if A has a join W A ∈ X, then W A ∈ Y .We say a morphism f : X1 → X2 of posets preserves existing joins (resp., existing meets) if for any subset A of X1 such that W A (resp., V A) exists in X1, the join W f (A) (resp., the meet V f (A)) exists in X2 and one has f (W A) = W f (A) (resp., 

f (V A) = W f (A)). 

Proposition 3.19. Let w ∈ W . Define Aw := { x ∈ W | Φx ∩ Φw−1 = ∅ } and 

Bw := { z ∈ W | Φw ⊆ Φz }, so 1W ∈ Aw and w ∈ Bw.(a) Aw and Bw are join-closed meet sub-semilattices of (W, ≤).(b) The map θw : Aw → Bw defined by θw(x) := wx is an order isomorphism. (c) θw and θ−1 

> w

each preserve all existing meets and joins of subsets of their domain. Proof. We prove (a). The fact Aw is closed under formation of meets in W of non-empty subsets of Aw is clear since Aw is a down-set of W . Also, if A ⊆ Aw and W A exists in ( W, ≤), then W A ∈ Aw by the JOP for ( W, ≤). The assertion for Bw

is clear since Bw is the principal up-set of W generated by w.Part (b) follows from basic properties of weak order (see 1.5), and (c) is a direct consequence of (b). □

4. Abstract root systems of Brink-Howlett groupoids 

Example 4.1 . Suppose that W is finite. Let J ⊆ S. The unique U ′ ∈ MJ containing 

U ∈ RJ is the parabolic closure of U , which is defined to be the intersection of all parabolic subgroups of W which contain U . One has U ′ ∈ PJ and Φ U ′ =span(Φ U ) ∩ Φ. It follows that MJ = PJ (for finite W ). In general, PJ̸ = RJ ; for instance, this is well known for ( W, S ) of type B2.Suppose instead that W is irreducible affine and J = {α} where α ∈ Π. Then MJ

consists of the rank two, finite parabolic subgroups containing s = sα ∈ S, together with the unique infinite maximal dihedral reflection subgroup W ′ containing s. In the standard realization of W as Euclidean reflection group, W ′ is generated by the reflections in the reflecting hyperplanes parallel (in the usual sense of Euclidean geometry) to the reflecting hyperplane for s. In particular, MJ ⊋ PJ in this case unless W is of rank two (that is, type eA1). 

Proposition 4.2. (a) PJ ⊆ MJ and QJ = MJ ∩ NJ .(b) Inclusions as shown in the following Hasse diagram hold amongst the sets
