Proof of Theorem 1.33. For each possible choice of X (i.e. R, M , N , P or Q) and each morphism ( K, w, J ) in G, and all U ∈ XJ , one has wU w −1 ∈ XK . Hence the formula for Υ X (K, w, J ) in the statement of the theorem defines a function 

XJ × {±} → XK × {±} . Straightforward calculation shows that the specified maps ΥX on objects and morphisms define a functor giving a signed G-set Υ X provided that for any morphisms ( K, x, J ) and ( L, y, K ) of G and all U ∈ XJ , we have 

η(yx, U, J ) ≡ η(y, xU x −1, K ) + η(x, U, J ) (mod 2) Recall that η(x, U, J ) is equal to 1 or 0 according as whether ( U \ WJ ) ∩ T is contained in, or disjoint from, N (x−1) (containment or disjointness are the only pos-sibilities, by Proposition 4.3). This implies similar descriptions of η(y, xU x −1, K )and η(yx, U, J ). Since WK = xW J x−1, that description of η(y, xU x −1, K ) implies that η(y, xU x −1, K ) is equal to 1 or 0 according as whether ( U \ WJ ) ∩ T is con-tained in, or disjoint from, x−1N (y−1)x. We relate η(yx, U, J ) to η(x, U, J ) and 

η(y, xU x −1, K ), by considering cases, using the 1-cocycle formula 

N (( yx )−1) = N (x−1y−1) = N (x−1) + x−1N (y−1)x

(in which, recall, + denotes symmetric difference of sets: A+B := ( A∪B)\(A∩B)). 

Case 1. η(x, U, J ) = 0 and η(y, xU x −1, K ) = 0. We have ( U \ WJ ) ∩ N (x−1) = ∅ and ( U \ WJ ) ∩ x−1N (y−1)x = ∅. The 1-cocycle formula implies ( U \ WJ ) ∩ N (( yx )−1) = ∅, so 

η(x, U, J ) + η(y, xU x −1, K ) = 0 = η(xy, U, J ).

Case 2. η(x, U, J ) = 0 and η(y, xU x −1, K ) = 1. We have ( U \ WJ ) ∩ N (x−1) = ∅ and ( U \ WJ ) ∩ T ⊆ x−1N (y−1)x. The 1-cocycle formula implies ( U \ WJ ) ∩ T ⊆ N (( yx )−1), so 

η(x, U, J ) + η(y, xU x −1, K ) = 1 = η(xy, U, J ).

Case 3. η(x, U, J ) = 1 and η(y, xU x −1, K ) = 0. We have ( U \ WJ ) ∩ T ⊆ N (x−1) and ( U \ WJ ) ∩ x−1N (y−1)x = ∅. The 1-cocycle formula implies ( U \ WJ ) ∩ T ⊆ N (( yx )−1), so 

η(x, U, J ) + η(y, xU x −1, K ) = 1 = η(xy, U, J ).

Case 4. η(x, U, J ) = 1 and η(y, xU x −1, K ) = 1. We have ( U \WJ )∩T ⊆ N (x−1) and ( U \WJ )∩T ⊆ x−1N (y−1)x. The 1-cocycle formula implies ( U \ WJ ) ∩ N (( yx )−1) = ∅, so 

η(x, U, J ) + η(y, xU x −1, K ) = 2 ≡ 0 = η(xy, U, J ) (mod 2) .50 M. DYER AND H. GIMENEZ

Corollary 4.5. Any inversion set for a signed groupoid set (G, ΥX ) as in Theorem 1.33 is a finite set. Proof. It is sufficient to prove that the inversion set Υ X,g = Υ X (K)+ ∩ g(Υ X (J)−)is finite, where g = ( K, w, J ) is a morphism in G. By Theorem 1.33, this inversion set consists of all roots ( U, +) where U ∈ XJ satisfies ( U \WJ )∩T ⊆ N (w−1). Recall 52 M. DYER AND H. GIMENEZ 

that N (w−1) is a finite set. Since U = ⟨ U ∩ T ⟩ and U ∩ T = ( WJ∩T ) ∪ (U \ WJ ) ∩ T ,it follows that there are only finitely many possibilities for U . □

4.6. Proposition 4.7 below will be used in studying realizations (of root systems of Brink-Howlett groupoids) with particularly favorable properties. In formulating it, we use the notation of 1.39. Let G be a connected Brink-Howlett groupoid of ( W, S ). For any object J ⊆ Πof G, let Σ J denote the union of Jinf with the set of all vertices of Π \ J which are joined (by an edge of the Coxeter graph of ( W, S )) either to a vertex of Jinf or to infinitely many vertices of Jfin . Let EJ be the subspace of V which consists of all vectors v in V such that B(v, β ) = 0 for all β ∈ Jinf and B(v, β )̸ = 0 for only finitely many β ∈ Jfin . Thus, Π \ ΣJ = Π ∩ EJ .

Proposition 4.7. Let notation be as in 4.6. (a) Let J, K ∈ ob( G). Then Jinf = Kinf , the symmetric difference Jfin + Kfin 

is finite, ΣJ = Σ K and EJ = EK . Then Σ′ := Jinf , Σ := Σ J and E := EJ

are well-defined (independent of J). Further, Σ′ ⊆ Σ and E ⊥ Σ′.(b) Φ′ := E ∩ Φ is a standard parabolic root subsystem of Φ with canonical simple system Π′ = E ∩ Π = Π \ Σ. Denote the corresponding Coxeter system as (W ′, S ′). Then W ′ = WΦ′ = WΠ′ , Φ′ = Φ W ′ , Π′ = Π W ′ and 

S′ = χ(W ′) = { sβ | β ∈ Π′ }.(c) There is a component G′, with ob( G′) = { Jfin | J ∈ ob( G) }, of the full Brink-Howlett groupoid of (W ′, S ′), and a groupoid isomorphism θ : G → G′

given by J 7 → Jfin on objects and (K, w, J ) 7 → (Kfin , w, J fin ) on morphisms, which induces a bijection between the set of Brink-Howlett generators of G

and that of G′.(d) Let (G, Λ) be as defined from (W, S ), Φ and G as in 1.21 and let (G′, Λ′) be defined analogously from (W ′, S ′), G′ and Φ′. Consider the real compres-sions (G, Λrec ) and (G′, (Λ ′)rec ). Then there is an equality of signed G-sets 

(G, Λrec ) = ( G, (Λ ′)rec ◦ θ).Remark. For K ∈ ob( G′), no vertex of the Coxeter graph of Π ′ is joined to infinitely many elements of K, and all components of the Coxeter graph of K are spherical. 

Proof. Since G is connected and generated by its Brink-Howlett generators, induc-tion reduces the proof of the assertions of (a) involving both J and K to the case in which there exists a Brink-Howlett generator ( K, w, J ) of G, say with w = ν(α, J ). Then w−1 = ν(K, β ) where J ˙∪ { α} = K ˙∪ { β}. Denote the component of J ∪ { α}

containing α as L ˙∪ { α} where L ⊆ J. Then L ∪ { α} is of finite type, so L is a union of finitely many components of Jfin . It follows that α̸ ∈ ΣJ and L ⊆ Jfin ⊆ Π \ ΣJ .Hence (4.7.1) w = wL∪{ α}wL ∈ WL∪{ α} ⊆ WΠ\ΣJ .

Since J \L ⊥ L∪{ α}, we have K = w(J) = w(J \L) ˙ ∪ w(L) = ( J \L) ˙ ∪ w(L) where 

w(L) ⊆ L ∪ { α} is of finite type and w(L) ⊥ (J \ L). This implies that Kinf = Jinf .Using + for symmetric difference, we therefore have 

Jfin + Kfin = ( Jfin + Jinf ) + ( K + Kinf ) = J + K

= (( J ˙∪ { α}) + {α}) + (( K ˙∪ { β}) + {β}) = {α} + {β}

which is finite. This proves the assertions of (a) involving both J and K. The other assertions of (a) follow directly using the definitions. ROOT SYSTEMS OF BRINK-HOWLETT GROUPOIDS 53 

The first assertion of (b) is proved by noting that Φ ′′ := Φ ∩ (Jinf )⊥ is a standard parabolic root subsystem of Φ by Proposition 1.41(a), and that, since Jfin ⊆ Φ′′ ∩Π, Φ′ = { β ∈ Φ′′ | B(β, γ )̸ = 0 for only finitely many γ ∈ Jfin }

is a standard parabolic root subsystem of Φ ′′ by Proposition 1.41(b). The rest of (b) follows using basic facts about reflection subgroups. Part (c) is from [9], but we give a proof for completeness. We claim first that for any morphism ( K, w, J ) of G, we have w ∈ W ′. It suffices to prove the claim in case (K, w, J ) is a Brink-Howlett generator of G, say with w = ν(α, J ) as in the proof of (a). In that proof, the component L ∪ { α} of J ∪ { α} is contained in Jfin ∪ { α},so it is the component of Jfin ˙∪ { α} containing α. Hence ν(α, J \ Σ) is defined in (W ′, S ′) and is equal to wL∪{ α}wL = w, by (4.7.1). This proves the claim. Note also that w(Jfin ) = Kfin since w(J) = K and w = ν(α, J ) = ν(α, L ) = ν(α, J fin ), so (Kfin , w, J fin ) is a Brink-Howlett morphism of the full Brink-Howlett groupoid H

of ( W ′, S ′). In fact, for any morphism ( K, w, J ) of G, we have w(J) = K and therefore 

w(Jfin ) = Kfin and w(Jinf ) = Kinf (i.e. w(Σ ′) = Σ ′). It follows from the claim that the maps J 7 → Jfin on objects and ( K, w, J ) 7 → (Kfin , w, J fin ) on morphisms define a homomorphism θ′ of groupoids from G to H. It is an embedding (that is, θ′ is injective both on objects and on morphisms) since for J ∈ ob( G), we have 

J = Jfin ˙∪ Jinf = Jfin ˙∪ Σ′. Moreover, the proof of the claim shows that θ′ sends Brink-Howlett generators of G to Brink-Howlett generators of H.To complete the proof of (c), it will suffice to show that if ( K′, w, J ′) is a Brink-Howlett generator of H, where J′ = θ(J) = Jfin with J ∈ ob( G), then there exists K in ob( G) such that θ′(K) = K′, ( K, w, J ) is a Brink-Howlett generator of G and θ′(K, w, J ) = ( K′, w, J ′). Write w = ν(α, J ′) with α ∈ Π′ \ J′ and let 

L ˙∪ { α}, with L ⊆ J′, denote the (spherical) component of J′ ˙∪ { α} which contains 

α. Now J = J′ ˙∪ Jinf where J′ = Jfin ⊥ Jinf and α ∈ Π′ = Π \ Σ so α ⊥ Jinf (in particular, α̸ ∈ Jinf ). Hence Jinf ⊥ L ˙∪ { α}. Thus, L ˙∪ { α} is the component of 

J ˙∪ { α} containing α, and ν(α, J ) = ν(α, L ) = ν(α, J ′) = w are all defined. Let 

K := w(J), so ( K, w, J ) is a Brink-Howlett generator of G and K ∈ ob( G). Then 

θ(K, w, J ) = ( θ(K), w, J fin ) = ( θ(K), w, J ′) is a morphism of H. Since ( K′, w, J ′)is also a morphism of H, we get K′ = θ(K), completing the proof of (c). The proof of (d) uses Proposition 3.8(a). For an object J of G, the result implies that the roots in Λ rec (J) are the subsets of Φ of the form w(Φ s), where ( J, w, K ) is a morphism of G and ( K, s, L ) is an atomic morphism (i.e. Brink-Howlett generator) of ( G, Λ). Such a root is positive if w(Φ s) ⊆ Φ+ and negative otherwise i.e. if 

w(Φ s) ⊆ Φ−. For a morphism ( L, u, J ) of G, one has (Λ rec (L, u, J ))( w(Φ s)) = (uw )(Φ s). This gives the equality as desired since, for any morphism ( K, s, L ) of G, atomic or not, we have s ∈ W ′, and for any s ∈ W ′, we have Φ ′ 

> s

Proof of Theorem 1.49(b). Suppose now that Π is linearly independent and that 

X = P . It suffices to show that each ιJ : ΥJ P → V (J) is an injection for each object J of G. Let us suppose that ( Ui, ϵ i) ∈ ΥJ P for i = 1 , 2 are such that 

ιJ (U1, ϵ 1) = ιJ (U2, ϵ 2). This means that ϵ1πJ (rU1,J ) = ϵ2πJ (rU2,J ). Hence rU1,J ∈

span( {rU2,J } ∪ Jfin ). Thus, we get that span(Π U1 ) = span(Π U2 ) where Π Ui =

{rUi,J } ∪ J for i = 1 , 2. Since we are assuming that Ui is parabolic and that Π is linearly independent, we have that Φ Ui = span(Π Ui ) ∩ Φ for i = 1 , 2. Thus, ΦU1 = span(Π U1 ) ∩ Φ = span(Π U2 ) ∩ Φ = Φ U2

and thus U1 = U2. Note that if ϵ1 and ϵ2 had opposite signs, then one would get 2 πJ (rU1,J ) = 0 and thus πJ (rU1,J ) = 0. But since WJ ⊊ U1, and because ΦJ = span( J) ∩ Φ, we must have that πJ (rU1,J )̸ = 0, a contradiction. Therefore, 

ϵ1 = ϵ2, and thus ιJ is injective for any object J of G. This proves part (b). □

Proof of Theorem 1.49(c). Suppose now that |J| = 1. Let J = {α}. Let ( Ui, ϵ i) ∈

ΥJ X for i = 1 , 2. Suppose that ιJ (U1, ϵ 1) = ιJ (U2, ϵ 2). That is, ϵ1πJ (rU1,J ) = 

ϵ2πJ (rU2,J ). If ϵ1̸ = ϵ2, then πJ (rU1,J ) = −πJ (rU2,J ), so πJ (rU1,J + rU2,J ) = 0, meaning that rU1,J + rU2,J = cα for some c ∈ R. Note that if c ≤ 0, then 

rU1,J + rU2,J + ( −c)α = 0 where the left hand side is a non-negative linear combination of positive roots, with at least one coefficient strictly positive. This contradicts the positive independence of Π. If c > 0, then 

c = cB (α, α ) = B(α, cα ) = B(α, r U1,J + rU2,J ) = B(α, r U1,J ) + B(α, r U2,J )where the two terms on the right hand side above are non-positive since {α, r U1,J }

and {α, r U2,J } are root bases. This contradicts c > 0. If c = 0, then rU1,J = −rU2,J 

contrary to the fact rUi,J is a positive root for i = 1 , 2. Thus, we must conclude that ϵ1 = ϵ2 and therefore πJ (rU1,J ) = πJ (rU2,J ). That is, rU1,J = rU2,J + cα for some c ∈ R. By Proposition 2.44(a), either rU1,J = rU2,J 

or rU2,J = sα(rU1,J ). We show that rU1,J = rU2,J in either case. To see this, suppose that sα(rU1,J ) = rU2,J . Because {α, r Ui,J } = Π Ui , we have B(α, r Ui,J ) ≤ 0for i = 1 , 2. Hence 0 ≥ B(α, r U2,J ) = B(α, s αrU1,J ) = B(sα(α), r U1,J ) = −B(α, r U1,J ) ≥ 0.

Thus, B(α, r U2,J ) = 0, and so rU1,J = sα(rU2,J ) = rU2,J . Hence rU1,J = rU2,J ,which implies that ( U1, ϵ ) = ( U2, ϵ ). Thus, we have that ιJ is injective for each object J with |J| = 1. This proves Theorem 1.49(c). □

Proof of Theorem 1.49(d). Assume X = N or X = Q. We need to establish that

Proof of Proposition 1.50. We prove (a). Let J be an object of G. By Proposition 2.30, we have span( Jfin ) ∩ Φ = Φ Jfin . If ( U, ϵ ) ∈ ΥX (J), then rU,J ∈ ΦU \ ΦJ ⊆

Φ\ΦJfin and therefore rU,J ̸ ∈ span( Jfin ) = ker( πJ ). Hence ιJ (U, ϵ ) = πJ (ϵr U,J )̸ = 0. This proves (a). We prove (b). We have Π = Jfin ∪ (Π \ Jfin ) where Jfin ⊆ ker( πJ ). Since πJ is linear, 

πJ (cone(Π)) = cone( πJ (Π)) = cone( πJ (Jfin ∪ (Π \ Jfin ))) = cone( πJ (Π \ Jfin )) = cone( ∆ J ),

proving (b). We prove (c). Let ( U, +) ∈ Υ+ 

> X

(J). Then ιJ (U, +) = πJ (rU,J ) ⊆ πJ (cone Π) = cone( ∆ J ) by (b). We conclude that ιJ (Υ + 

> X

(J)) ⊆ cone( ∆ J ), proving (c). We prove (d). Let x ∈ cone( ∆ J ) ∩ − cone( ∆ J ). Note that, by (b), we have cone( ∆ J ) = πJ (cone(Π \ Jfin )). Hence x = πJ (y) = −πJ (z) for some elements 

y, z ∈ cone(Π \ Jfin ). Hence y + z ∈ cone(Π \ Jfin ). We also have y + z ∈ ker( πJ ) = span( Jfin ) since πJ (y+z) = x−x = 0. Hence y+z ∈ cone(Π \Jfin )∩span( Jfin ) = {0},by Proposition 2.30(e). Since y, z ∈ cone(Π), this implies that y = z = 0, by positive independence of Π. Hence x = 0, completing the proof of (d). Parts (e)–(f) follow from the definitions by elementary linear algebra. □

6. Bilinear forms of realized Brink-Howlett groupoids 

6.1. We say that the form B is positive definite on a subspace U of V if the restriction of B to U is positive definite i.e. if B(v, v ) > 0 for all non-zero v ∈ U .For any subset L of Π, define the subspace VL of V by 

VL := { v ∈ V | B(v, α )̸ = {0} for only finitely many α ∈ L }.

Proposition 6.2. Let L ⊆ Π.(a) If L is finite, then VL = V .(b) If α ∈ Φ ∩ VL, then B(α, β )̸ = 0 for only finitely many β ∈ L.(c) If every component of L is of finite type, then VL = span( L) ⊕ L⊥ where 

span( L) and L⊥ are B-orthogonal. (d) If any one of the conditions 1.53(i)–(iii) holds for some object J of a con-nected Brink-Howlett groupoid G, then it holds for every object of G.Proof. Parts (a)–(b) are trivial. We prove (c). Clearly, span( L) and L⊥ are B-orthogonal for any subset L of Π. Assume every component of L is of finite type. Then in particular, B is positive definite on span( L), which implies that the sum span( L) + L⊥ is direct, since for 

v ∈ span( L) ∩ L⊥, we have 0 ≤ B(v, v ) ∈ B(span( L), L ⊥) ⊆ { 0}

and hence v = 0. Clearly, L⊥ ⊆ VL, and we have span( L) ⊆ VL since every component of L is finite. Hence span( L) + L⊥ ⊆ VL.To complete the proof of (c), it remains to show that if v ∈ VL, then v ∈

span( L) + L⊥. Let J be the union of all components of L which contain a root which is not orthogonal to v. By the assumptions, J is a finite union of components of L, so span( J) ⊥ span( L \ J). We also have v ⊥ span( L \ J) by the definition of J. Since all components of L are of finite type finite, J is a spherical subset of Π. Since B is positive definite on span( J), we may choose a basis v1, . . . , v n of span( J) such that B(vi, v j ) = δi,j (the Kronecker delta). Let v′ = Pni=1 B(v, v i)vi ∈

span( J) ⊆ span( L) and v′′ := v − v′. We have B(v, v i) = B(v′, v i) for i = 1 , . . . , n ,so v′′ = v − v′ ∈ J⊥. Also, v, v ′ ∈ (L \ J)⊥ so v′′ ∈ (L \ J)⊥. Hence v′′ ∈ L⊥,

v′ ∈ span( L) and v = v′ + v′′ , as required. To prove (d), let ( K, w, J ) be a morphism of a Brink-Howlett groupoid G. We are to show that if J satisfies any one of the conditions 1.53(i)–(iii), then that same condition holds with J replaced by K. For (i) and (ii), this follows since, by Proposition 4.7(a), the symmetric difference Jfin + Kfin is finite, and Jinf = Kinf .For (iii), it suffices by (c) to show that VJfin = VKfin ; this holds since the definitions imply that VL = VL′ if L, L ′ ⊆ Π with L + L′ finite. □

6.3. As shown in [9], the study of connected Brink-Howlett groupoids G in gen-eral can be reduced to the case when each object J of G satisfies the conditions 1.53(i)–(ii). Propositions 4.7–4.9 show that the study of such a groupoid G and its (abstract) real roots can be reduced to the situation when each object J of G

satisfies 1.53(i)–(iii); Theorem 1.49(d) then implies that the corresponding systems of real roots can be faithfully realized in real vector spaces. These conditions lead 60 M. DYER AND H. GIMENEZ 

to useful extra structure, such as an analogue of the W -invariant bilinear form B

on V , and some gains in simplicity of statements, with little loss for many purposes, and we impose them henceforward. 

Assumption 6.4. For the remainder of this paper, we assume unless otherwise stated that each object J of G satisfies conditions 1.53(i)–(iii). We consider the weakly realized signed groupoid set ( G, ΥX , V, ι ) where X denotes either P , M or R, and adopt the associated notation and identifications described in 1.55–1.54. In partic-ular, for an object J of J, πJ denotes the orthogonal projection V → V (J) = J⊥.6.5. Let F be a subset of Π such that F = Ffin and V = span( F ) + F ⊥. Let 

πF : V → F ⊥ denote the orthogonal projection determined by this orthogonal direct sum. This extends the notation already introduced in case F ∈ ob( G). The projection πF may be alternatively described as follows. Given β ∈ V ,choose a finite subset M of F such that M is a union of components of F and 

{ α ∈ F | B(α, β )̸ = 0 } ⊆ M . Then M is a spherical subset of Π, and (6.5.1) πF (β) = 1

|WM |

X

> w∈WM

wβ. 

We leave the verification of this formula to the reader. Note that it implies πF (β) = 

πM (β), as can easily be checked by other means (e.g. using Proposition 6.7(a) below). We may sometimes write F ⊥ as VF or, if F ∈ ob( G), as VF . For notational compactness, we shall often write vF := πF (v) for v ∈ V .Subsets F ⊆ Π such that F = Ffin and V = span( F ) ⊕ F ⊥ include any F ⊆ Πsuch that WF is a finite index overgroup of WJ where J is an object of G, and any spherical subset F of Π. In particular, they include the sets K and L = J ∪ K

where J ∈ ob( G) and K ⊆ Π is such that ν(K, J ) is defined. We shall require below some properties of the projection πL, for suitable L ⊆ Π, which we prove by checking the existence of a suitable analogue of “fundamental weights” for L in span( L). The proof reduces to the case where L is finite and irreducible, in which case the relevant properties are known from Vinberg’s lemma ([32, Ch 4]), Perron-Frobenius theory (see for example [36]) or [5, Ch 5, §3, no. 5]. If β ∈ L, we write Lβ for the component of L containing β.

Proof of Proposition 1.58. Part (a) is clear from the discussion above and Proposi-tion 1.50. We prove (b). Let α, β ∈ ∆J . Write α = πJ (α′) and β = πJ (β′) where α′, β ′ ∈

Part (c) also follows using Proposition 6.10(c), and (d) follows from (c) using Theorem 1.33 and Proposition 1.35. In (e), it is sufficient to prove the two unions there are of disjoint sets. Disjoint-ness in the first union holds for any signed groupoid set, and that in the second union follows since ∆J ⊆ eΥJ X . Part (f) follows from Proposition 1.50(e). □

Proof of Proposition 1.60. We prove (a). Let K ⊆ Π \ J. Write the union of all components of K ∪ J which intersect K as L ˙∪K. Note L ⊆ J is the union of all components of J which contain a vertex which is joined to a vertex of K in the Coxeter graph of Π. Recall that ν(K, J ) exists if and only L ∪ K is spherical, in which case K ∪ L is linearly independent. If ν(K, J ) exists, then K is certainly finite. So there is no loss of generality in assuming for the proof of (a) that K is finite. Since K ⊥ J \ L, we have 

πJ (K) = πL(K) ⊆ span( K ∪ L). Since L is spherical, the restriction of B to span( L) is positive definite and there is a B-orthogonal direct sum span( K ∪ L) = span( L)⊕span( πL(K)). It follows that the restriction of B to span( K∪L) is positive definite (i.e. K ∪ L is spherical) if and only if the restriction of B (or equivalently, of BJ ) to span( πL(K)) = span( πJ (K)) is positive definite. This proves (a). For the proof of (b)–(f), assume that w := ν(K, J ) exists, and let L be as in the proof of (a). Since K ∪ L is spherical, it is linearly independent. From the proof of (a), 

|L| + |K| = dim(span( L ∪ K)) = dim(span( L)) + dim(span( πL(K))) = |L| + dim(span( πL(K))) .

Hence dim(span( πJ (K))) = dim(span( πJ (K))) = |K|, so πJ (K) is linearly inde-pendent, proving (b). We prove (c). We have w = ν(K, J ) = ν(K, L ) = wK∪LwL and ν(α, J ) =

Proposition 7.2. For any object J of the groupoid G, we have CJ = C ∩ J⊥ where 

C := { v ∈ V | B(v, α ) ≥ 0 for all α ∈ Π }.Proof. Let x ∈ CJ . Then x ∈ VJ = J⊥. Hence B(x, J ) ⊆ { 0}. Also by definition of CJ , we have B(x, π J (α)) ≥ 0 for all α ∈ Π \ J. Since πJ (α) ∈ α + span( J) where 

B(x, span( J)) = {0}, this gives B(x, α ) ≥ 0 for all α ∈ Π \ J. Hence B(x, Π) ⊆ R≥0

and x ∈ C ∩ J⊥. This proves CJ ⊆ C ∩ J⊥.To prove the reverse inclusion, let x ∈ C ∩ J⊥. Then x ∈ J⊥ = VJ . Further, since 

x ∈ C , we have B(x, Π \ J) ⊆ B(x, Π) ⊆ R≥0. Hence B(x, α ) ≥ 0 for all α ∈ Π \ J.But since πJ (α) ∈ α + span( J) and x ∈ J⊥, it follows that B(x, α ) = B(x, π J (α)) for all α ∈ Π \ J. Thus, we get B(x, π J (α)) ≥ 0 for all α ∈ Π \ J, so we deduce that 

x ∈ CJ . This proves that CJ = C ∩ J⊥. □

Proof of Theorem 1.67. Part (a) follows from Proposition 7.2. Since F := Π ∩ A⊥ and A ⊆ C, part (b) follows from Proposition 2.18. For part (c), let K, L be objects of H. Let ( L, w, K ) be a morphism of G′.Then w ∈ WF , and since WF stabilizes A pointwise, we conclude that V(L, w, K )stabilizes A pointwise. Thus, ( L, w, K ) is a morphism of H. Conversely, if ( L, w, K )is a morphism of H, then V(L, w, K ) fixes A pointwise, so by part (b), w ∈ WF ,and thus w is a morphism of G′. This proves (c). For part (d), let K be an object of H. By definition of H, we have A ⊆ K⊥.Note that K is also an object of the full subgroupoid G′. Let L be another object of 

G′ that lies in the same component as K. Since L and K lie in the same component of G′, it follows that there exists a morphism ( L, w, K ) of G′ where w ∈ WF .We claim that L ⊆ A⊥. Let β ∈ L. There exists α ∈ K such that β = w(α). Since F = Π ∩ A⊥, we have span( F ) ⊆ A⊥. Also, α ∈ K ⊆ A⊥ since A ⊆ K⊥.Hence β = w(α) ∈ α + span( F ) ⊆ A⊥, for all β ∈ L. Thus, L ⊆ A⊥ as claimed. This claim implies that A ⊆ L⊥, so L is an object of H. We have shown that if 

L is an object of G′ that lies in the same component of G′ as some object K (where 

K is an object of H), then L is also an object of H. This fact along with what we proved in part (c) proves that H is a union of components of G′. □

Now consider the general case, with Π possibly linearly dependent. We sketch an argument involving compatibility of the construction of realized root systems of Brink-Howlett groupoids with lifts, to reduce to the case in which Π is linearly independent. The argument is straightforward but somewhat lengthy, and we omit many routine details. Consider a universal lift L : bD → D of D = ( V, B, Φ, Π) where bD = ( bV , bB, bΦ, bΠ), defined as in 2.34 (we write here bX instead of eX as in 2.34, for typographical reasons). In particular, bΠ is a basis of bV , by definition of universal lifts. Denote the associated Coxeter system of the based root system ( bΦ, bΠ) as ( cW , bS). The canonical identification cW → W , denoted as bw 7 → w, makes L : bV → V a W -equivariant map and preserves bilinear forms. Also, L restricts to bijections bΦ → Φ and bΠ → ΠMany notions X we have defined for ( W, S ) from (Φ , Π) on ( V, B ) have analogues, which we will denote bX, defined in the same way for ( cW , bS) from ( bΦ, bΠ) on ( bV , bB). For example, for any J ⊆ Φ, we write bJ = { α ∈ bΦ | L(α) ∈ J }. Corresponding to the Brink-Howlett groupoid G of ( W, S ), there is a Brink-Howlett groupoid bG

of ( cW , bS) with objects { bJ | J ∈ ob( G) } and morphisms ( bK, bw, bJ) for morphisms (K, w, J ) of G.More importantly for the argument here, there is a realized signed groupoid set ( bG, bΥX , bV, bι ), where X denotes R, M or P , defined in the same way as ( G, ΥX , V, ι ). The assumed validity of conditions 1.53(i)–(iii) for ( G, V, Π) implies their validity for ( bG, bV , bΠ); to prove this, one observes that, in Proposition 6.2, if K ⊆ Π and 74 M. DYER AND H. Henceforward, we identify ( W, S ) with ( cW , bS) and thereby ( bG, bΥX ) = ( G, ΥX ), for notational simplicity. Let J ∈ ob( G).
